8.
Lemmata for extangential LEQs

For this section,
OABC
denotes a non-kite extangential quadrilateral with consecutive sides
a,b,c,d
and with its excircle outside the vertex
B
, so
a>c
. In particular, it has exradius
r_{e}=\frac{2(a+b)}{a-c}
. As explained in Remark
12
, we may assume
a=\max\{a,b,c,d\}
and
b=\min\{a,b,c,d\}
.

The approach adopted in this section is the same as that of Section
3
, and the results obtained are analogous, but the calculations are often more complicated.
For the convenience of the reader, we repeat Equations (
12
) and (
13
):

(69)

\displaystyle p^{2}=a^{2}+b^{2}\pm 2\sqrt{a^{2}b^{2}-(2K_{A})^{2}},

(70)

\displaystyle q^{2}=a^{2}+d^{2}\pm 2\sqrt{a^{2}d^{2}-(2K_{O})^{2}},

where
p,q
are the lengths of the diagonals
OB,AC
respectively. As
O,A,B,C
are lattice points,
p^{2},q^{2}
are integers, so by Lemma
1
, the integers
a^{2}b^{2}-(2K_{A})^{2}
and
a^{2}d^{2}-(2K_{O})^{2}
are squares.

Lemma 9
.

One has

p^{2}=\frac{8(a+b)(K_{A}-K_{C})}{(a-c)(a-d)}+(a+b)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad q^{2}=\frac{8(K_{O}-K_{B})}{a-c}+(a-d)^{2}.

Proof.

As
OABC
is not a kite, by hypothesis, so
a\not=d
. Arguing exactly as in Proposition
6
we reobtain (
67
):

(a-c)(a-d)p^{2}=4(K_{A}^{2}-K_{C}^{2})+(a-d)(a-c)(a+b)^{2},

and since
OABC
is extangential and hence not a parallelogram,
a\not=c
. Thus, as
K_{A}^{2}-K_{C}^{2}=(K_{A}+K_{C})(K_{A}-K_{C})=2(a+b)(K_{A}-K_{C})
,
we obtain the required formula for
p^{2}
.

Similarly, by applying Heron’s formula to triangles
OAC
and
BCA
, we obtain

(71)

2(d^{2}-c^{2}+a^{2}-b^{2})q^{2}=16(K_{O}^{2}-K_{B}^{2})+(d^{2}-a^{2})^{2}-(b^{2}-c^{2})^{2}.

Simplifying as in Proposition
6
gives

(a-c)(a+b)q^{2}=8(a+b)(K_{O}-K_{B})+(a+b)(a-c)(a-d)^{2},

from which the required formula for
q^{2}
follows.
∎

Remark 13
.

From the above lemma, using (
69
),

\frac{8(a+b)(K_{A}-(a+b))}{(a-c)(a-d)}=\frac{p^{2}-(a+b)^{2}}{2}=-ab\pm\sqrt{a^{2}b^{2}-(2K_{A})^{2}},

which is an integer by Lemma
1
. Similarly,
\frac{8(K_{O}-(a+b))}{a-c}
is an integer.

Lemma 10
.

The integer

abcd-4(a+b)^{2}

is a square, and

\displaystyle K_{A}

\displaystyle=(a+b)+(a-c)(a-d)(a+b)\frac{-(ab+cd)\pm 2\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}}{16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2}},

\displaystyle K_{O}

\displaystyle=(a+b)+(a-c)\frac{ad+bc\mp 2\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}}{16+(a-c)^{2}},

where the signs of the square roots in the formulas for
K_{O}
and
K_{A}
are opposite.

Remark 14
.

In the statement of the above lemma, the terms

ab+cd\pm 2\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad ad+bc\mp 2\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}

are strictly positive. Indeed, using
d=a+b-c
, by the arithmetic mean-geometric mean inequality,

ab+cd\geq 2\sqrt{abcd}>\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}
.
In particular,
K_{A}<\frac{K}{2}=a+b
if and only if
a>d
, which is opposite to the situation for tangential LEQs; see Remark
4
.

Proof of Lemma
10
.

From Lemma
9
and Equation (
70
),

\frac{4(K_{O}-K_{B})}{a-c}-ad=\frac{q^{2}-a^{2}-d^{2}}{2}=\pm\sqrt{a^{2}d^{2}-(2K_{O})^{2}},

so setting
s:=\frac{K_{O}-(a+b)}{a-c}
, squaring, and using
K_{O}+K_{B}=2(a+b)
gives

\alpha s^{2}-2\beta s+\gamma=0,

where

\alpha=16+(a-c)^{2},\quad\beta=ad+bc,\quad\gamma=(a+b)^{2}.

Thus, as
\beta^{2}-\alpha\gamma=4(abcd-4(a+b)^{2})
(using
a+b=c+d
again), we have

s=\frac{ad+bc\pm 2\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}}{16+(a-c)^{2}},

which gives the required formula for
K_{O}
. In particular, as
s
is rational,
abcd-4(a+b)^{2}
is a square, as claimed.

The formula for
K_{A}
is similarly obtained by equating
p^{2}
from Lemma
9
and Equation (
69
). We have

\frac{4(a+b)(K_{A}-K_{C})}{(a-c)(a-d)}+ab=\frac{p^{2}-a^{2}-b^{2}}{2}=\pm\sqrt{a^{2}b^{2}-(2K_{A})^{2}}.

Define
t:=\frac{(a+b)(K_{A}-(a+b))}{(a-c)(a-d)}
. One obtains
\bar{\alpha}t^{2}+2\bar{\beta}t+\bar{\gamma}=0
,
where

\bar{\alpha}=16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2},\quad\bar{\beta}=(a+b)^{2}(ab+cd),\quad\bar{\gamma}=(a+b)^{4}.

One has

\bar{\beta}^{2}-\bar{\alpha}\bar{\gamma}=4(a+b)^{4}(abcd-4(a+b)^{2}),

so

t=\frac{-(a+b)^{2}(ab+cd)\pm 2(a+b)^{2}\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}}{16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2}},

which gives the required formula for
K_{O}
.

It remains to see that the signs of the square roots in the formulas for
K_{O}
and
K_{A}
are opposite.
Let
R=2\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}
. Obviously, we may assume that
R\not=0
and
a\not=c
. Let us write

\displaystyle K_{A}

\displaystyle=(a+b)+(a-c)(a-d)(a+b)\frac{-(ab+cd)+\delta_{A}R}{16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2}},

\displaystyle K_{O}

\displaystyle=(a+b)+(a-c)\frac{ad+bc+\delta_{O}R}{16+(a-c)^{2}},

where
\delta_{A},\delta_{O}
are each
\pm 1
. Using
a+b=c+d
,

\displaystyle K_{A}-

\displaystyle\frac{(a+b)(a-b)}{a-c}

\displaystyle=(a+b)\left(\frac{b-c}{a-c}+(a-c)(a-d)\frac{-(ab+cd)+\delta_{A}R}{16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(a+b)(d-a)}{a-c}\cdot\frac{16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(ac+bd)-(a-c)^{2}\delta_{A}R}{16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2}},

\displaystyle K_{O}-

\displaystyle\frac{(a+b)(a+d)}{a-c}=-\frac{(a+b)(c+d)}{a-c}+(a-c)\frac{ad+bc+\delta_{O}R}{16+(a-c)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{a-c}\cdot\frac{16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(ac+bd)-(a-c)^{2}\delta_{O}R}{16+(a-c)^{2}}.

Notice also that
(a+b)(d-a)=bd-ac
. Hence, by Proposition
9
,

(72)

\frac{X-(a-c)^{2}\delta_{A}R}{16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2}}\cdot\frac{X-(a-c)^{2}\delta_{O}R}{16+(a-c)^{2}}=(a+b)^{2},

where
X=16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(ac+bd)
. Now, substituting
d=a+c-b
one finds that

\frac{X-(a-c)^{2}\delta_{A}R}{16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2}}\cdot\frac{X+(a-c)^{2}\delta_{A}R}{16+(a-c)^{2}}=(a+b)^{2}.

Subtracting from (
72
) gives

(73)

(X-(a-c)^{2}\delta_{A}R)\cdot(a-c)^{2}(\delta_{A}+\delta_{O})R=0.

Note that
X-(a-c)^{2}\delta_{A}R\not=0
as otherwise
X^{2}=(a-c)^{4}R^{2}
which would give

(16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(ac+bd))^{2}-(a-c)^{4}(abcd-4(a+b)^{2})=0,

and hence

(a+b)^{2}(16+(a-c)^{2})(16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(b-c)^{2})=0,

which is impossible. So from (
73
), we have
\delta_{A}=-\delta_{O}
, as claimed.
∎

Lemma 11
.

The sign of the square root in the formulas for
K_{O}
is positive if and only if
B
lies within the circumcircle of the triangle
OAC
; in particular, the sign for
K_{O}
is positive if
OABC
is concave.

Proof.

In the notation of the above proof, let
x=\delta_{O}2\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}
, so

\displaystyle K_{A}

\displaystyle=(a+b)+(a-c)(a-d)(a+b)\frac{-(ab+cd)-x}{16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2}},

\displaystyle K_{O}

\displaystyle=(a+b)+(a-c)\frac{ad+bc+x}{16+(a-c)^{2}}.

From a standard criteria for a point to be within the circumcircle of a triangle (see
[
15
]
),

B
is inside the circumcircle of the triangle
OAC
if and only if

(74)

p^{2}K_{O}<d^{2}K_{A}+a^{2}K_{C}.

Now
d^{2}K_{A}+a^{2}K_{C}=K_{A}(d^{2}-a^{2})+2a^{2}(a+b)
, and by Lemma
9
,

K_{O}p^{2}=K_{O}\left(\frac{16(a+b)(K_{A}-(a+b))}{(a-c)(a-d)}+(a+b)^{2}\right).

So condition (
74
) can be written as
E>0
where

E=K_{A}(d^{2}-a^{2})+2a^{2}(a+b)-K_{O}\left(\frac{16(a+b)(K_{A}-(a+b))}{(a-c)(a-d)}+(a+b)^{2}\right).

Substituting the formulas for
K_{O},K_{A}
and
x
, one finds upon simplification that

E=2\delta_{O}(a+b)\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}.

Hence, as claimed,
\delta_{O}>0
if and only if

B
is inside the circumcircle of the triangle
OAC
.
∎

Definition 5
.

Let

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle=8\cdot\frac{ad+bc+2\delta\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}}{16+(a-c)^{2}},

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=8(a+b)^{2}\cdot\frac{ab+cd+2\delta\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}}{16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2}},

where
\delta=1
if
B
lies within the circumcircle of the triangle
OAC
, and
\delta=-1
otherwise.

Remark 15
.

Observe that
\Sigma
and
T
are positive integers. Indeed,
from Lemma
10
,

(75)

\Sigma=\frac{8(K_{O}-(a+b))}{a-c},\qquad T=\frac{8(a+b)((a+b)-K_{A})}{(a-c)(a-d)},

which are integers by Remark
13
, and they are positive by Remark
14
. Furthermore, from Lemma
2
,

(76)

\Sigma=\frac{1}{2}(q^{2}-(a-d)^{2}),\qquad T=\frac{1}{2}((a+b)^{2}-p^{2}).

In the notation of the proof of Lemma
10
,

(77)

\alpha\Sigma^{2}-16\beta\Sigma+64\gamma=0,

where

\alpha=16+(a-c)^{2}
,

\beta=ad+bc
,

\gamma=(a+b)^{2}=(c+d)^{2}
,
and

(78)

\bar{\alpha}T^{2}-16\bar{\beta}T+64\bar{\gamma}=0,

where

\bar{\alpha}=16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2}
,

\bar{\beta}=(a+b)^{2}(ab+cd)
,
and
\bar{\gamma}=(a+b)^{4}
.

Lemma 12
.

The following relations hold:

(a)

\Sigma T=8\frac{(a+b)^{2}}{(a-c)^{2}}(T-{\Sigma})
,

(b)

2\Sigma T=(a+b)^{2}(\Sigma-8)-(b-c)^{2}T
.

Proof.

(a). As in the proof of Lemma
11
, let
x=2\delta\sqrt{abcd-4(a+b)^{2}}
. Then, cross-multiplying, the required identity is

E=0
, where

\displaystyle E=

\displaystyle(ad+bc+x)\big(16(a+b)^{2}+(a-c)^{2}(a-d)^{2}\big)

\displaystyle-(a+b)^{2}(ab+cd+x)\big(16+(a-c)^{2}\big)+(a-c)^{2}(ad+bc+x)(ab+cd+x).

Expanding and using
d=a+c-b
, one has

E=(a-c)^{2}\big(4(4(a+b)^{2}-abcd)+x^{2}\big).

Then replacing
x^{2}
by
4(abcd-4(a+b)^{2})
gives

E=0
, as required.

(b). From the definitions, since
(a-c)^{2}=(a-c)(d-b)=ad+bc-ab-cd
,

\displaystyle(a+b)^{2}(16+(a-c)^{2})\Sigma

\displaystyle-(16(a+b)^{2}+(b-c)^{2}(a-c)^{2})T

\displaystyle=8(a+b)^{2}(ad+bc-ab-cd)=8(a+b)^{2}(a-c)^{2}.

Part (b) follows by applying part (a).
∎

Remark 16
.

As
\Sigma
and
T
are positive, Lemma
12
(a) gives
\Sigma<T
.
Furthermore, as
T-\Sigma<T
, Lemma
12
(a) gives
\Sigma<8\frac{(a+b)^{2}}{(a-c)^{2}}
, and Lemma
12
(b) gives

2T<(a+b)^{2}\frac{\Sigma-8}{\Sigma}<(a+b)^{2}
. In particular,
\Sigma>8
.

Remark 17
.

From (
75
),

(79)

\displaystyle K_{O}

\displaystyle=(a+b)+\frac{1}{8}(a-c)\Sigma,

(80)

\displaystyle K_{A}

\displaystyle=(a+b)-\frac{(a-c)(a-d)}{8(a+b)}T.

Then by Proposition
10
, the parameter
\lambda_{e}
and the exradius
r_{e}
are related to
\Sigma
by

(81)

\lambda_{e}\cdot\Sigma=8\frac{(a+b)^{2}}{(a-c)^{2}}=2r_{e}^{2}.

Using Lemma
12
(a), we can also write

(82)

\lambda_{e}=\frac{T}{T-\Sigma}.

Remark 18
.

We have the non-degeneracy condition
K_{B}\not=0
as otherwise
ABC
would be colinear. Thus
K_{O}\not=2(a+b)
and (
79
) gives

\Sigma\not=8\frac{a+b}{a-c}
. Hence, by Lemma
12
(a), we have

(83)

\Sigma(T-\Sigma)\not=8T.

Notice also that
OABC
is concave if and only if
K_{O}>2(a+b)
, that is, from (
75
), when

\Sigma>8\frac{a+b}{a-c}
.

Lemma 13
.

(c-b)T<(a-b)\Sigma
.

Proof.

From the assumption that the vertices
O,A,B,C
are positively oriented and if
OABC
is concave, the reflex angle is at
B
, we have
K_{A}>0
. So (
80
) gives

8(a+b)^{2}>(a-c)(a-d)T
.
Lemma
12
(a) gives
8(a+b)^{2}=(a-c)^{2}\frac{\Sigma T}{T-\Sigma}
. Hence, as
T-\Sigma>0
by Remark
16
, and using
a+b=c+d
, we obtain

(a-c)\Sigma>(c-b)(T-\Sigma)
.
Rearranging this gives the required result.
∎

Lemma 14
.

\Sigma
and
T
both divide
8(a+b)^{2}
.

Proof.

From
77
,

\frac{1}{16}\alpha\Sigma^{2}-\beta\Sigma+4\gamma=0
,
where

\alpha=16+(a-c)^{2}
,

\beta=ad+bc
,

\gamma=(a+b)^{2}
.
So
\frac{1}{16}\alpha\Sigma^{2}
is an integer, and hence
\frac{1}{16}(a-c)^{2}\Sigma^{2}
is an integer. So
\frac{1}{4}(a-c)\Sigma
is an integer, and hence
\frac{1}{4}\alpha\Sigma
is an integer. Hence, as

16(a+b)^{2}=16\gamma=(-\frac{1}{4}\alpha\Sigma+4\beta)\Sigma,

\Sigma
divides
16(a+b)^{2}
. So if
\Sigma
is odd, then
\Sigma
divides
(a+b)^{2}
. If
\Sigma
is even, then as
\frac{1}{32}\alpha\Sigma^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta\Sigma+2\gamma=0
, so

\frac{1}{32}\alpha\Sigma^{2}
is an integer, and hence
\frac{1}{32}(a-c)^{2}\Sigma^{2}
is an integer. It follows that
\frac{1}{64}(a-c)^{2}\Sigma^{2}
is an integer. So
\frac{1}{8}(a-c)\Sigma
is an integer, and hence
\frac{1}{8}\alpha\Sigma
is an integer. Hence, as

8(a+b)^{2}=(-\frac{1}{8}\alpha\Sigma+2\beta)\Sigma,

\Sigma
divides
8(a+b)^{2}
.

By Lemma
12
(a), we have
8(a+b)^{2}(\frac{1}{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{T})=(a-c)^{2}
. Since
8(a+b)^{2}\frac{1}{\Sigma}
is an integer, it follows that
8(a+b)^{2}\frac{1}{T}
is also an integer.
∎

9.
Explicit examples of extangential LEQs

In this section we exhibit non-kite extangential LEQs in the three cases with
(\Sigma,T)
equal to
(9,18),(18,50)
and
(45,50)
respectively. As before, let us define
h:=\frac{a+b}{a-c}
, so
h=\sqrt{\frac{\Sigma T}{8(T-\Sigma)}}
, by Lemma
12
(a). Note that
h>1
, but as we will see,
h
may fail to be an integer.

We have

(84)

b=(h-1)a-hc.

In particular,
b>0
gives
a>\frac{h}{h-1}c
and since
b\leq c
, we have
a\leq\frac{h+1}{h-1}c
.
From (
77
),
\alpha\Sigma^{2}-16\beta\Sigma+64\gamma=0
,
where

\alpha=16+(a-c)^{2}
,

\beta=ad+bc
,

\gamma=(a+b)^{2}=(c+d)^{2}
. Using (
84
), substituting
d=a+b-c
and solving for
a
gives

a=\frac{64ch^{2}-16c\Sigma+c\Sigma^{2}\pm 4\sqrt{2c^{2}\Sigma(8h^{2}-\Sigma)(\Sigma-8)-(\Sigma-8h)^{2}\Sigma^{2}}}{(\Sigma-8h)^{2}}.

We claim that
\frac{64ch^{2}-16c\Sigma+c\Sigma^{2}}{(\Sigma-8h)^{2}}>\frac{h+1}{h-1}c
.
Indeed, cross multiplying and simplifying, the claim is
(8h^{2}-\Sigma)(\Sigma-8)>0
, which is true since
\Sigma<8h^{2}
by Remark
16
and
\Sigma>8
, also by Remark
16
.
Hence, since
a\leq\frac{h+1}{h-1}c
, it follows that

(85)

a=\frac{64ch^{2}-16c\Sigma+c\Sigma^{2}-4\sqrt{2c^{2}\Sigma(8h^{2}-\Sigma)(\Sigma-8)-(\Sigma-8h)^{2}\Sigma^{2}}}{(\Sigma-8h)^{2}}.

We first classify the extangential LEQs with
(\Sigma,T)=(9,18)
.
Note that this is one of two cases in Theorem
3
(a).
Suppose we have an extangential LEQ with
a>c\geq b
and
\Sigma=9,T=18
. So
h=\sqrt{\frac{\Sigma T}{8(T-\Sigma)}}=\frac{3}{2}
,
and from (
84
),

(86)

b=\frac{1}{2}(a-3c).

Hence
a>3c
and since
b\leq c
, we have
a\leq 5c
. From (
85
),

(87)

a=9c-4\sqrt{2c^{2}-9}.

Working modulo 3, the fact that
2c^{2}-9
is a square gives us that
c
is divisible by 3, say
c=3v
. Then
v
satisfies the negative Pell equation

(88)

u^{2}-2v^{2}=-1,

for some positive integer
u
. Then
(
86
) and (
87
) give

(89)

(a,b,c,d)=3(9v-4u,3v-2u,v,11v-6u).

It is well known that the solutions
(u_{j},v_{j})
to (
88
) are given recursively by

(90)

u_{j+1}=3u_{j}+4v_{j},\qquad v_{j+1}=2u_{j}+3v_{j},

with initial values
(u_{1},v_{1})=(1,1)
.

We now define the vertices of our quadrilaterals.
Let

\displaystyle A_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\left(9u_{j}-8v_{j}-7,9u_{j}-8v_{j}+7\right),

\displaystyle B_{j}

\displaystyle=6\left(3u_{j}-3v_{j}-2,3u_{j}-3v_{j}+2\right)

\displaystyle C_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\left(11u_{j}-12v_{j}-7,11u_{j}-12v_{j}+7\right).

Note that
A_{j},B_{j},C_{j}
are lattice points as
u_{j},v_{j}
are odd, as one can see from the recursive formula (
90
).
We will consider the quadrilateral
OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
. Let
a_{j},b_{j},c_{j},d_{j}
denote the lengths of the sides
OA_{j},A_{j}B_{j},B_{j}C_{j},C_{j}O_{j}
respectively.
The distance
a_{j}
is given by

\displaystyle a_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{9}{4}((9u_{j}-8v_{j}-7)^{2}+(9u_{j}-8v_{j}+7)^{2})=\frac{9}{2}(81u_{j}^{2}-144u_{j}v_{j}+64v_{j}^{2}+49)

\displaystyle=\frac{9}{2}(81u_{j}^{2}-144u_{j}v_{j}+64v_{j}^{2}+49(2v_{j}^{2}-u_{j}^{2}))\quad\text{(by \eqref{P1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{9}{2}(32u_{j}^{2}-144u_{j}v_{j}+162v_{j}^{2})

\displaystyle=9(9v_{j}-4u_{j})^{2}.

So
a_{j}=3(9v_{j}-4u_{j})
. Similarly, the other side lengths are as follows:

b_{j}=3(3v_{j}-2u_{j}),\qquad c_{j}=3v_{j},\qquad d_{j}=3(11v_{j}-6u_{j}),

as anticipated by (
89
). So the perimeter of
OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
is

a_{j}+b_{j}+c_{j}+d_{j}=36(2v_{j}-u_{j})
.

The area of
OA_{j}B_{j}
is

\frac{1}{2}\|\overrightarrow{OA_{j}}\times\overrightarrow{OB_{j}}\|=9(5v_{j}-3u_{j})
.
The area of
OB_{j}C_{j}
is

\frac{1}{2}\|\overrightarrow{OB_{j}}\times\overrightarrow{OC_{j}}\|=9(3v_{j}-u_{j})
.
Notice in passing that as the signed areas of
OA_{j}B_{j}
and
OB_{j}C_{j}
are both positive, as one can see recursively using (
90
), so
OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
has no self-intersection. The area of
OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
is

9(5v_{j}-3u_{j})+9(3v_{j}-u_{j})=36(2v_{j}-u_{j})
, so
OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
has equal area and perimeter, i.e., it is equable.
Thus
OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
is a LEQ.
Furthermore,
OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
is extangential because
a_{j}+b_{j}=c_{j}+d_{j}
.

The first member of this family, corresponding to the initial condition
(u_{1},v_{1})=(1,1)
, is the kite with side lengths
15,3,3,15
shown in Figure
9
. The vertices and side lengths of the next four members of this family are given in Table
4
.
The case
(u_{2},v_{2})=(7,5)
of the family is shown in Figure
11
.

The exradius of
OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
is

r_{e,j}=\frac{K(OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j})}{a_{j}-c_{j}}
. Substituting the values gives
r_{e,j}=3
for each
j
.
Then by Proposition
8
, the
excenter is

I_{e,j}=\frac{3}{2}\,\frac{a_{j}C_{j}+d_{j}A_{j}}{K(OA_{j}C_{j})}
,
which simplifies to

15(1,-1)+21(u_{j}+7v_{j})(1,1)
. In particular, the excenter of each family member is a lattice point.
By Proposition
10
, the coordinate
\lambda_{e,j}
of the excenter is

\lambda_{e,j}=\frac{3(a_{j}+b_{j})}{2K(OA_{j}C_{j})-3(a_{j}-c_{j})}
. Substituting the values gives

\lambda_{e,j}=2
for each
j
.

Remark 19
.

By Remark
18
, an extangential LEQ
OABC
is concave if and only if

\Sigma>8\frac{a+b}{a-c}
; that is,
\Sigma>8h
. For the above family, with
(\Sigma,T)=(9,18)
, we have

h=\frac{3}{2}
. So all members of this family are convex.

Now suppose we have an extangential LEQ with
a>c\geq b
and
\Sigma=18,T=50
.
Note that this is the other case in Theorem
3
(a).
So
h=\sqrt{\frac{\Sigma T}{8(T-\Sigma)}}=\frac{15}{8}
and (
84
) gives

(91)

b=\frac{1}{8}(7a-15c).

Hence
a>15/7c
and since
b\leq c
, we have
a\leq 23c/7
. From (
85
),

(92)

a=29c-12\sqrt{5c^{2}-4}.

So
c
satisfies the Pell-like equation

(93)

u^{2}-5c^{2}=-4,

for some positive integer
u
. Then
(
91
) and (
92
) give

(94)

(a,b,c,d)=(29c-12u,\frac{1}{2}(47c-21u),c,\frac{1}{2}(103c-45u)).

From
[
27
]
, the solutions to (
93
) are

(u_{j},c_{j})=(L_{2j-1},F_{2j-1})
,
where
L_{j}
is the
j
-th
Lucas number and
F_{j}
is the
j
-th Fibonacci number. (Recall that the Lucas and Fibonacci numbers satisfy the same recurrence relation but with different initial conditions:
F_{1}=F_{2}=1
while
L_{1}=1,L_{2}=3
). Hence (
94
) gives the potential solutions

(95)

(a_{j},b_{j},c_{j},d_{j})=F_{2j-1}\big(29,\frac{47}{2},1,\frac{103}{2}\big)-L_{2j-1}\big(12,\frac{21}{2},0,\frac{45}{2}\big).

From Lemma
13
,

T(c-b)<\Sigma(a-b)
.
So
50(c-b)<18(a-b)
and thus from (
91
) and (
92
),

51c>23u
,
which is

(96)

51F_{2j-1}>23L_{2j-1}.

Now
F_{6}=8,L_{6}=18
and
F_{7}=13,L_{7}=29
, and thus
51F_{6}<23L_{6}
and
51F_{7}<23L_{7}
. It follows from the Lucas and Fibonacci recurrence relation that (
96
) only holds for
j=1,2,3
. For
i=1,2
one finds using (
95
) that
a_{j}<d_{j}
, contrary to our hypothesis. So the only possibility is
j=3
, which gives the solution
(a,b,c,d)=(13,2,5,10)
, which is the concave LEQ shown on the right of Figure
10
.

Now suppose we have an extangential LEQ with
a>c\geq b
and
\Sigma=45,T=50
.
Note that this is the case
m=3
in Theorem
3
(b).
So
h=\sqrt{\frac{\Sigma T}{8(T-\Sigma)}}=\frac{15}{2}
and (
84
) gives

(97)

b=\frac{1}{2}(13a-15c).

Hence
a>15/13c
and since
b\leq c
, we have
a\leq 17c/13
. From (
85
),

(98)

a=\frac{1}{5}(109c-12\sqrt{74c^{2}-25}).

So
c
satisfies the Pell-like equation

(99)

W^{2}-74c^{2}=-25,

for some positive integer
W
. The solutions to this equation are not readily enumerated, and moreover, some solutions do not result in LEQs. For example, for the solution
W=5927,c=689
, one obtains the non-integer value
a=\frac{3977}{5}
from (
98
). We will restrict ourselves to constructing a particular infinite family of LEQs for which
c
is divisible by
5
. Set
w=5u,c=5v
, so that (
98
) gives the negative Pell equation
u^{2}-74v^{2}=-1
. Let us denote its solutions
(u_{j},v_{j})
, where
(u_{1},v_{1})=(43,5)
and
(u_{2},v_{2})=(318157,36985)
.
So

(100)

u_{j}^{2}-74v_{j}^{2}=-1.

The solutions
(u_{j},v_{j})
are well known; see entries A228546 and A228547 in
[
35
]
. In particular, they satisfy the second order recurrence relation

(101)

X_{j+2}=7398X_{j+1}-X_{j}.

It follows from this recurrence relation and the initial conditions that
u_{j}
is divisible by 43 and
v_{j}
is divisible by 5 for all
j
. Let
x_{j}=u_{j}/43,y_{j}=v_{j}/5
, so

(102)

43^{2}x_{j}^{2}-74\cdot 25\,y_{j}^{2}=-1,

and
(x_{1},y_{1})=(1,1)
and
(x_{2},y_{2})=(7399,7397)
.
Note that
(x_{j},y_{j})
also satisfies the recurrence relation (
101
). We have
c_{j}=25y_{j}
and from (
98
),
a_{j}=\frac{1}{5}(109c_{j}-60u_{j})=-12\cdot 43x_{j}+545y_{j}
. Then from (
97
),
b_{j}=\frac{1}{2}(13a_{j}-15c_{j})=-3354x_{j}+3355y_{j}
. Thus, as
d_{j}=a_{j}+b_{j}-c_{j}
,

(103)

(a_{j},b_{j},c_{j},d_{j})=-x_{j}(516,3354,0,3870)+y_{j}(545,3355,25,3875).

In particular,
(a_{j},b_{j},c_{j},d_{j})
are determined by the recurrence relation (
101
) with
(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1},d_{1})=(29,1,25,5)
and
(a_{2},b_{2},c_{2},d_{2})=(213481,689,184925,29245)
. The first three members of this family are shown in Table
5
.

Side lengths of three members of the

We have shown above that if an extangential LEQ with side lengths
a,b,c,d
has
(\Sigma,T)=(45,50)
and
c
is divisible by 5, then
(a,b,c,d)=(a_{j},b_{j},c_{j},d_{j})
for some
j
, where
(a_{j},b_{j},c_{j},d_{j})
is given by (
103
).
We now show that conversely, for each
j
, the 4-tuples
(a_{j},b_{j},c_{j},d_{j})
given by (
103
) are realised by the side lengths of an extangential LEQ.
We mention in passing that the difficulty in determining suitable vertices is two-fold: firstly, the quadrilaterals in this family grow so fast that we only had three examples to base our study on, and secondly, the pattern of the vertex coordinates is considerably more complicated than in the
(\Sigma,T)=(8,18)
family exhibited above.

To define the vertices, we will employ the following two first-order recurrence relations in two variables

(104)

\displaystyle x_{j+1}

\displaystyle=78x_{j}+25y_{j},\qquad y_{j+1}=25x_{j}+8y_{j},

(105)

\displaystyle x_{j+1}

\displaystyle=68x_{j}+35y_{j},\qquad y_{j+1}=35x_{j}+18y_{j},

under various initial conditions.
We identify the 2-tuple
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with the Gaussian integer
x+yi\in\mathbb{C}
, and we use the notations interchangeably, according to convenience. Consider the following families, for all
j\geq 1
:

(a)

z_{a,j}=x_{a,j}+y_{a,j}i
satisfies (
104
) with
z_{a,1}=5+2i
,

(b)

z_{b,j}=x_{b,j}+y_{b,j}i
satisfies (
104
) with
z_{b,1}=i
,

(c)

z_{c,j}=x_{c,j}+y_{c,j}i
satisfies (
105
) with
z_{c,1}=2+i
,

(d)

z_{d,j}=x_{d,j}+y_{d,j}i
satisfies (
105
) with
z_{d,1}=1
.

Let
\rho:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}
denote the reflection in the line
y=x
, so
\rho(x,y)=(y,x)
or equivalently
\rho(z)=i\bar{z}
, where
\bar{z}
denotes the complex conjugate of
z
. Let
\rho^{j}
denote the
j
-th iterate of
\rho
under composition, so
\rho^{j}=\rho
if
j
is odd and
\rho^{j}=\operatorname{id}
otherwise.
We now define the vertices.
Set

\displaystyle A_{j}

\displaystyle=\rho^{j+1}z_{a,j}^{2},\quad B_{j}=A_{j}+\rho^{j+1}z_{b,j}^{2},\quad C_{j}=5\rho^{j}z_{d,j}^{2},\quad B^{\prime}_{j}=C_{j}+5\rho^{j}z_{c,j}^{2}.

The first three members of this family are shown in Table
6
; the LEQ given in the first row is shown on the left of Figure
10
.

A_{j}

B_{j}

C_{j}

(21, 20)

(20, 20)

(0, 5)

(124080,173719)

(124480,174280)

(16995,23800)

(1285155641, 917968320)

(1289303420, 920931020)

(176054900, 125753505)

Table 6.
Vertices of three members of the
(\Sigma,T)=(45,50)
family

We will establish the following properties:

(i)

B_{j}=B^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j
,

(ii)

The areas
K_{A_{j}},K_{C_{j}}
of triangles
OA_{j}B_{j}
and
B_{j}C_{j}O
are positive (and hence
OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
is a non-self-intersecting, positively oriented quadrilateral),

(iii)

OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
has the side lengths
a_{j},b_{j},c_{j},d_{j}
given by (
103
),

(iv)

a_{j}+b_{j}=c_{j}+d_{j}
; i.e.,
OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
is extangential,

(v)

a_{j}+b_{j}+c_{j}+d_{j}=K_{A_{j}}+K_{C_{j}}
; i.e.,
OA_{j}B_{j}C_{j}
is equable.

The proofs of these properties will use the following technical results.

Lemma 15
.

Suppose the sequence
z_{j}=(x_{j},y_{j})
satisfies either (
104
) or (
105
). Then for all
j\geq 1
,

(a)

z_{j+2}=86z_{j+1}+z_{j}
,

(b)

|z^{2}_{j+2}|=7398\,|z^{2}_{j+1}|-|z^{2}_{j}|
(i.e.,
|z^{2}_{j}|
satisfies (
101
)),

(c)

z^{2}_{a,j+2}=7398\,z^{2}_{a,j+1}-z^{2}_{a,j}+(-1)^{j}(17500-24500i)
,

(d)

z^{2}_{b,j+2}=7398\,z^{2}_{b,j+1}-z^{2}_{b,j}+(-1)^{j}(2500-3500i)
,

(e)

z^{2}_{c,j+2}=7398\,z^{2}_{c,j+1}-z^{2}_{c,j}-(-1)^{j}(700-500i)
,

(f)

z^{2}_{d,j+2}=7398\,z^{2}_{d,j+1}-z^{2}_{d,j}-(-1)^{j}(4900-3500i)
.

Proof.

(a). It is easy to see that if
z_{j}
(regarded as a 2-tuple) satisfies the relation
z_{j+1}=Mz_{j}
, where

M=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix},

then
z_{j+2}=\operatorname{tr}(M)z_{j+1}-\det(M)z_{j}
.
The result follows as
68+18=78+8=86
and
68\cdot 18-35^{2}=78\cdot 8-25^{2}=-1
.

(b). From (a) we have
|z^{2}_{j+2}|=(86x_{j+1}+x_{j})^{2}+(86y_{j+1}+y_{j})^{2}
, so

|z^{2}_{j+2}|=86^{2}\,|z^{2}_{j+1}|+|z^{2}_{j}|+172(x_{j+1}x_{j}+y_{j}y_{j}).

Assume first that (
104
) holds. Then using (
104
) twice,

\displaystyle|z^{2}_{j+1}|-|z^{2}_{j}|

\displaystyle=(x_{j+1}^{2}+y_{j+1}^{2})-(x_{j}^{2}+y_{j}^{2})

\displaystyle=(68^{2}+35^{2}-1)x_{j}^{2}+(35^{2}+18^{2}-1)y_{j}^{2}+2\cdot(68+18)\cdot 35x_{j}y_{j}

\displaystyle=86(68x_{j}^{2}+70x_{j}y_{j}+18y_{j}^{2})

\displaystyle=86(x_{j+1}x_{j}+y_{j+1}y_{j}),

so, from above,

|z^{2}_{j+2}|=(86^{2}+2)|z^{2}_{j+1}|-|z^{2}_{j}|=7398\,|z^{2}_{j+1}|-|z^{2}_{j}|
, as required.
A similar argument applies when (
105
) holds.

(c). From (a) we have
z^{2}_{a,j+2}=7396\,z^{2}_{a,j+1}+172z_{a,j+1}z_{a,j}+z^{2}_{a,j}
. So we are required to show that

172z_{a,j+1}z_{a,j}+2z^{2}_{a,j}=2z^{2}_{a,j+1}+(-1)^{j}(17500-24500i)
, or equivalently, using (
104
),

5x_{a,j}^{2}-14x_{a,j}y_{a,j}-5y_{a,j}^{2}=35(-1)^{j}
,
for all
j
. In matrix notation, the condition is
z_{j}Qz_{j}^{t}=35(-1)^{j}
, where
z_{j}=(x_{a,j},y_{a,j})
,
z^{t}
denotes the transpose of
z
, and

Q=\begin{pmatrix}5&-7\\
-7&-14\end{pmatrix}.

One verifies readily that this condition holds for
j=1
, where
z_{1}=(5,2)
. Now using (
104
) and setting
M=\begin{pmatrix}78&25\\
25&8\end{pmatrix}
,
we have
z_{j+1}Qz_{j+1}^{t}=(z_{j}M)Q(Mz_{j}^{t})=-z_{j}Qz_{j}^{t}
, since

MQM=-Q
. So the required result follows by induction. This proves (c).

Part (d) is proven in the same manner; only the initial condition is different. For parts (e) and (f), one repeats the argument using the matrices

Q=\begin{pmatrix}7&-5\\
-5&-7\end{pmatrix},\qquad M=\begin{pmatrix}68&35\\
35&18\end{pmatrix}.

Once again, the argument works because
MQM=-Q
.
∎

Remark 20
.

It is well known that if a sequence
r_{j}
satisfies a second-order homogenous recurrence relation, then

r_{j}^{2}
satisfies a third-order recurrence relation, but in certain exceptional circumstances,
r_{j}^{2}
may satisfy a second-order recurrence relation, which is typically non-homogeneous
[
10
]
. In this respect, Lemma
15
is perhaps somewhat surprising.

(i). We have
B_{1}=(5+2i)^{2}+i^{2}=20+20i
and
B^{\prime}_{1}=5\rho((2+i)^{2}+1^{2})=B_{1}
, while

B_{2}=\rho((78\cdot 5+25\cdot 2+(25\cdot 5+8\cdot 2)i)^{2}+(25+8i)^{2})=124480+174280i
,
and
B^{\prime}_{2}=5((68\cdot 2+35+(35\cdot 2+18)i)^{2}+(68+35i)^{2})=B_{2}
.
From Lemma
15
(c) and (d), we have for all
j
,

\displaystyle B_{j+2}

\displaystyle=7398\rho(B_{j+1})-B_{j}+(-1)^{j}\rho^{j+1}(17500-24500i+2500-3500i)

\displaystyle=7398\rho(B_{j+1})-B_{j}+(-1)^{j}\rho^{j+1}(20000-28000i),

and from Lemma
15
(e) and (f), we have

\displaystyle B^{\prime}_{j+2}

\displaystyle=7398\rho(B^{\prime}_{j+1})-B_{j}-(-1)^{j}5\rho^{j}(700-500i+4900-3500i)

\displaystyle=7398\rho(B_{j+1})-B_{j}-(-1)^{j}\rho^{j}(28000-20000i)=B_{j+2}.

Hence
B_{j}=B^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j
.

(ii). Regarding
A_{j}
and
B_{j}
as complex numbers, one has
K_{A_{j}}=\frac{i}{4}(A_{j}\overline{B_{j}}-\overline{A_{j}}B_{j})
. Hence when
j
is odd,

K_{A_{j}}=\frac{i}{4}(z^{2}_{a,j}\bar{z}^{2}_{b,j}-\bar{z}^{2}_{a,j}z^{2}_{b,j})=(x_{a,j}y_{b,j}-x_{b,j}y_{a,j})(x_{a,j}x_{b,j}+y_{a,j}y_{b,j}),

while when
j
is even,

\displaystyle K_{A_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{4}(\rho(z^{2}_{a,j})\rho(\bar{z}^{2}_{b,j})-\rho(\bar{z}^{2}_{a,j})\rho(z^{2}_{b,j})=\frac{i}{4}(\bar{z}^{2}_{a,j}z^{2}_{b,j}-z^{2}_{a,j}\bar{z}^{2}_{b,j})

\displaystyle=-(x_{a,j}y_{b,j}-x_{b,j}y_{a,j})(x_{a,j}x_{b,j}+y_{a,j}y_{b,j}).

It is easy to see that
x_{a,j}x_{b,j}+y_{a,j}y_{b,j}>0
.
Moreover,
x_{a,j}y_{b,j}-x_{b,j}y_{a,j}
is the area
K_{j}
of the parallelogram
P_{j}
spanned by
z_{a,j}
and
z_{b,j}
. So we are required to show that
(-1)^{j+1}K_{j}>0
for all
j
. For
j=1
, one has
K_{1}=5
. Furthermore,
P_{j+1}
is the image of
P_{j}
under the linear transformation with matrix

M=\begin{pmatrix}78&25\\
25&8\end{pmatrix}
, which has determinant
-1
. Hence
K_{j}=(-1)^{j+1}5
, giving the required result.

A similar reasoning applies for
K_{C_{j}}
. Here

K_{C_{j}}=(-1)^{j}25\,\frac{i}{4}(z^{2}_{c,j}\bar{z}^{2}_{d,j}-\bar{z}^{2}_{c,j}z^{2}_{d,j})=(-1)^{j}25\,(x_{c,j}y_{d,j}-x_{d,j}y_{c,j})(x_{c,j}x_{d,j}+y_{c,j}y_{d,j}).

Setting
K_{j}=x_{c,j}y_{d,j}-x_{d,j}y_{c,j}
, one argues as before, using
K_{1}=-4
and the fact that
\begin{pmatrix}68&35\\
35&18\end{pmatrix}
has determinant
-1
.

(iii). As observed above, the lengths
(a_{j},b_{j},c_{j},d_{j})
are determined by the recurrence relation (
101
) with
(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1},d_{1})=(29,1,25,5)
and
(a_{2},b_{2},c_{2},d_{2})=(213481,689,184925,29245)
.
The side
OA_{j}
has length
|z_{a,j}^{2}|
. Hence, as
|z_{a,1}^{2}|=29
and from (
104
),

|z_{a,2}^{2}|=(78\cdot 5+25\cdot 2)^{2}+(25\cdot 5+8\cdot 2)^{2}=213481
, so Lemma
15
(b) gives
|OA_{j}|=a_{j}
for all
j
. By the same reasoning, the sides
A_{j}B_{j},B_{j}C_{j},C_{j}O
have lengths
b_{j},c_{j},d_{j}
respectively.

(iv). We have
a_{j}+b_{j}=|z_{a,j}^{2}|+|z_{b,j}^{2}|
and
c_{j}+d_{j}=(|z_{c,j}^{2}|+|z_{d,j}^{2}|)
. So
a_{j}+b_{j}
and
c_{j}+d_{j}
satisfy the recurrence relation (
101
), by Lemma
15
(b). Hence, since
a_{1}+b_{1}=30=c_{1}+d_{1}
and
a_{2}+b_{2}=214170=c_{2}+d_{2}
, we have
a_{j}+b_{j}=c_{j}+d_{j}
for all
j
, as required.

(v). As we saw above,
a_{j}+b_{j}+c_{j}+d_{j}
satisfies recurrence relation (
101
) and
a_{1}+b_{1}+c_{1}+d_{1}=60
and

a_{2}+b_{2}+c_{2}+d_{2}=428340
. From above,

K_{A_{j}}=(-1)^{j+1}\frac{i}{4}(z^{2}_{a,j}\bar{z}^{2}_{b,j}-\bar{z}^{2}_{a,j}z^{2}_{b,j}),\qquad K_{C_{j}}=(-1)^{j}25\,\frac{i}{4}(z^{2}_{c,j}\bar{z}^{2}_{d,j}-\bar{z}^{2}_{c,j}z^{2}_{d,j}).

One finds easily that
K_{A_{1}}+K_{C_{1}}=60
and
K_{A_{1}}+K_{C_{1}}=428340
. So it remains to show that
K_{A_{j}}+K_{C_{j}}
satisfies (
101
). In fact, we will show that
K_{A_{j}}
and
K_{C_{j}}
both satisfy (
101
).
As we saw in the proof of property (ii),

K_{A_{j}}=(-1)^{j+1}(x_{a,j}y_{b,j}-x_{b,j}y_{a,j})(x_{a,j}x_{b,j}+y_{a,j}y_{b,j}),

and
(-1)^{j+1}(x_{a,j}y_{b,j}-x_{b,j}y_{a,j})=5
. So we will show that
r_{j}:=x_{a,j}x_{b,j}+y_{a,j}y_{b,j}
satisfies (
101
). Using (
104
) three times, we have

\displaystyle r_{j+2}

\displaystyle=6709x_{a,j+1}x_{b,j+1}+2150x_{b,j+1}y_{a,j+1}+2150x_{a,j+1}y_{b,j+1}+689y_{a,j+1}y_{b,j+1}

\displaystyle=49633181x_{a,j}x_{b,j}+15905700x_{b,j}y_{a,j}+15905700x_{a,j}y_{b,j}+5097221y_{a,j}y_{b,j}

\displaystyle=7398r_{j+1}-r_{j},

as required. The proof that
K_{C_{j}}
satisfies (
101
) is obtained in exactly the same manner, using (
105
).

Remark 21
.

We have just determined all extangential LEQs with side lengths
a,b,c,d
that have
(\Sigma,T)=(45,50)
and
c
is divisible by 5. However, there are extangential LEQs with
(\Sigma,T)=(45,50)
for which
c
is not divisible by 5. Here are two examples: the LEQ with vertices
A=(6300,4505),\ B=(6320,4520),\ C=(861,620)
and side lengths
7745,25,6709,1061
, and the LEQ with vertices
A=(33303495,46624900),\ B=(33410980,46775380),\ C=(4562280,6387199)
and side lengths
57297505,\ 184925,\ 49633181,\ 7849249
.

Remark 22
.

By Remark
18
, an extangential LEQ
OABC
is concave if and only if

\Sigma>8\frac{a+b}{a-c}=8h
. As we saw above, for extangential LEQs with
(\Sigma,T)=(45,50)
, we have

h=\frac{15}{2}
. So all extangential LEQs with
(\Sigma,T)=(45,50)
are convex.

10.
Theorem
3
from Theorem
4

As in the previous section, let
OABC
be a non-kite extangential LEQ with sides
a,b,c,d
and with its excircle outside the vertex
B
. As explained in Remark
12
, we may assume
a=\max\{a,b,c,d\}
and
b=\min\{a,b,c,d\}
.
We introduce some new variables:

Definition 6
.

Let

x=a+b,y=a-c,z=c-b
. By Lemma
14
,
\Sigma
and
T
both divide
8x^{2}
. Define
k
by
8x^{2}=kT
.
By Remark
16
,
\Sigma<T
. Let
\Sigma^{\prime}=T-\Sigma
.

From our previous observations we now extract four important consequences.

(a)

By Lemma
12
(b),
16\Sigma x^{2}=kx^{2}(\Sigma-8)-8x^{2}z^{2}
, so
\Sigma=\frac{8(k+z^{2})}{k-16}
. In particular,
k>16
.

(b)

By Lemma
12
(a),
8x^{2}\Sigma^{\prime}=y^{2}\Sigma T=\frac{1}{k}8x^{2}y^{2}\Sigma
, so
\Sigma^{\prime}=\frac{y^{2}\Sigma}{k}
and thus
T=\Sigma+\Sigma^{\prime}=\frac{(k+y^{2})\Sigma}{k}
.

(c)

From the definitions,
x=\sqrt{\frac{k(\Sigma+\Sigma^{\prime})}{8}}
.

(d)

By Lemma
13
,

(c-b)T<(a-b)\Sigma
, so by (b),
z(k+y^{2})<k(y+z)
and hence
yz<k
.

It follows that the hypotheses of Theorem
4
are satisfied. So one of the following holds:

(a)

(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})=(9,9),(12,24),(16,16),(24,12),(10,40),(40,10)
or
(18,32)
,

(b)

(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})=(5m^{2},5)
for some integer
m
for which there exists integers
n,Y,Z
such that
m^{2}-10n^{2}=-1
and
(5m^{2}-8)Y^{2}=5+8Z^{2}
,

(c)

(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})=(m^{2},1)
for some integer
m
for which there exists integers
n,Y,Z
such that
m^{2}-2n^{2}=-1
and
(m^{2}-8)Y^{2}=1+8Z^{2}
.

In case (a), we have

(\Sigma,T)=(9,18),(12,36),(16,32),(24,36),(10,50),(40,50)\ \text{or}\ (18,50).

Note that in the cases
(\Sigma,T)=(12,36),(16,32),(24,36),(10,50),(40,50)
, we have

\Sigma(T-\Sigma)=8T
, so these are all degenerate cases which are excluded by Remark
18
.
Thus
(\Sigma,T)=(9,18)
or
(18,50)
, as required.

In case (b), we have

(\Sigma,T)=(5m^{2},m^{2}+5)
, and in case (c), we have

(\Sigma,T)=(m^{2},m^{2}+1)
, as required.

11.
Proof of Theorem
4

The proof of Theorem
4
will occupy us for most of the rest of this paper.
The general strategy is to analyse the different possibilities for the ratio
\Sigma^{\prime}/\Sigma
.
Suppose
\Sigma^{\prime}=\frac{u}{v}\Sigma
, where
\gcd(u,v)=1
. So hypothesis (b) gives
vy^{2}=uk
. Then the assumption
k>yz
gives
vy^{2}>uyz
so
vy>uz
.
From (c) we have

(106)

x=\sqrt{\frac{k(\Sigma+\Sigma^{\prime})}{8}}=\sqrt{\frac{y^{2}(u+v)\Sigma}{8u}}.

Hypothesis (a) gives
\Sigma(vy^{2}-16u)=8(uz^{2}+vy^{2})
.

Throughout this section, we use the following notation.

Definition 7
.

For an integer
n
, we let
f(n)
denote the square-free part of
n
, and write
n=f(n)s^{2}(n)
.

Since
vy^{2}=uk
and
\gcd(u,v)=1
,
we have that
f(u)s(u)
divides
y
, say
y=f(u)s(u)y^{\prime}
. So
vy>uz
gives

(107)

vy^{\prime}>s(u)z.

Furthermore,
y^{2}=f(u)uy^{\prime 2}
and hypothesis (a) can now be rewritten as

(108)

\Sigma(vf(u)y^{\prime 2}-16)=8(z^{2}+vf(u)y^{\prime 2}),

and from (
106
) we have

(109)

x=\sqrt{\frac{y^{\prime 2}f(u)(u+v)\Sigma}{8}}.

We split the problem up into 6 cases:

1.

u
is odd,
v
is even and the 2-adic order of
v
is even.

2.

u
is odd,
v
is even and the 2-adic order of
v
is odd.

3.

u
is even,
v
is odd and the 2-adic order of
u
is even.

4.

u
is even,
v
is odd and the 2-adic order of
u
is odd.

5.

u
and
v
are both odd and the 2-adic order of
u+v
is even.

6.

u
and
v
are both odd and the 2-adic order of
u+v
is odd.

In each case, we make several change of variables. These will be introduced as we go along, but for the convenience of the reader, we summarize the main variables in Table
7
.

Case

\Sigma

w

y

z

1

2

Variable changes

Case 1. Assume
u
is odd,
v
is even and the 2-adic order of
v
is even.

We will show that in this case,
(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})=(40,10)
is the only possibility.

As
x
is an integer, and as
u,u+v
are relatively prime odd integers,
from (
109
) we can write
\Sigma=2f(u+v)f(u)w^{2}
, for some
w
.
Note
v
divides
\Sigma
as
v\Sigma^{\prime}=u\Sigma
and
\gcd(u,v)=1
. So
v
divides
2w^{2}
, and thus as the 2-adic order of
v
is even,
v
divides
w^{2}
. Hence
f(v)s(v)
divides
w
. Thus, setting
w=f(v)s(v)w^{\prime}
we have
\Sigma=2f(u+v)f(u)f(v)vw^{\prime 2}

and (
108
) gives

(110)

f(u+v)f(u)f(v)vw^{\prime 2}(vf(u)y^{\prime 2}-16)=4(z^{2}+vf(u)y^{\prime 2}).

Thus
vf(u)
divides
4z^{2}
, so
f(v)s(v)f(u)
divides
2z
, say
2z=f(v)s(v)f(u)z^{\prime}
. So (
107
) gives
2vy^{\prime}>s(u)f(v)s(v)f(u)z^{\prime}

and hence

(111)

2s(v)y^{\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime},

and (
110
) gives

(112)

f(u+v)f(v)w^{\prime 2}(vf(u)y^{\prime 2}-16)=f(v)f(u)z^{\prime 2}+4y^{\prime 2}.

Hence
f(v)
divides
4y^{\prime 2}
. Since the 2-adic order of
v
is even,
f(v)
is odd, so
f(v)
divides
y^{\prime}
. Let
y^{\prime}=f(v)y^{\prime\prime}
.
Then (
111
) gives

2f(v)s(v)y^{\prime\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime}
,
and (
112
) gives

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=f(u)z^{\prime 2}+4f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}
.
From this last equation, notice that as
v
is even and
f(u)
is odd,
z^{\prime}
must be even, say
z^{\prime}=2z^{\prime\prime}
. Then (
111
) gives

(113)

f(v)s(v)y^{\prime\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime\prime}\quad\text{and so}\quad vf(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}>uf(u)z^{\prime\prime 2},

and (
112
) gives

(114)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4(f(u)z^{\prime\prime 2}+f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}).

Note that from the left-hand side of (
114
), we have

(115)

vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}>16.

Furthermore, (
113
) and (
114
) give

uf(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)<4(v+u)f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}.

Hence

(116)

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{4}{f(v+u)}\left(\frac{1}{uf(u)f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)f(u)}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16}\right).

A slightly weaker but useful consequence is

(117)

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{uf(u)f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)f(u)}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16}\right).

We will use (
116
) and (
117
) repeatedly to derive contradictions with the fact that, being a positive integer,
w^{\prime}\geq 1
.
Note that (
116
) is only useful when we know something about
f(u+v)
, so (
117
) will be more commonly applied. Even so, we sometimes only have information about
uf(u)
, and not about
f(u)
, for which we use the trivial bound
f(u)\geq 1
.

Remark 23
.

The first twelve possible values of
v
are shown in Table

. Notice that the sequence
vf(v)
is not monotonically increasing in

. By hypothesis,
v
is divisible by 4, so

.

(a)

if

, then
v\geq 12,vf(v)\geq 16
and

,

(b)

if

, then either

or
vf(v)\geq 36
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 36
,

(c)

if
vf^{2}(v)\geq 36
, then either
v=36
or
vf(v)\geq 64
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 64
,

(d)

if
vf^{2}(v)\geq 64
, then either
v=64
or
vf(v)\geq 100
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 100
.

Note also that if
u>1
, then
u\geq 3
and
uf(u)\geq 9
.

Lemma 16
.

Either
u=1
or
v=4
or
y^{\prime\prime}=1
.

Proof.

It suffices to note that if
u>1,v>4
and
y^{\prime\prime}>1
, then (
117
) would give

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{9}+\frac{1}{16}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{16\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{25}{27}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

∎

Lemma 17
.

y^{\prime\prime}\not=1
.

Proof.

Suppose
y^{\prime\prime}=1
. Then (
113
) gives
vf(v)>uf(u)z^{\prime\prime 2}\geq uf(u)
. Also, from (
115
),
vf^{2}(v)f(u)>16
. So if
v=4
, then
vf(v)=vf^{2}(v)=4
and hence
f(u)>4
, contradicting the fact that
vf(v)>uf(u)
. Hence
v>4
and so, by Remark
23
,
vf^{2}(v)\geq 16
. By Remark
23
again, if
vf^{2}(v)=16
, then
v=16
, in which case
vf^{2}(v)f(u)>16
gives
f(u)\geq 3
, so
u\geq 3
and
uf(u)\geq 9
. Then (
117
) would give

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<4\left(\frac{1}{9}+\frac{1}{48}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{48-16}\right)=\frac{19}{24}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Thus
vf(v)>16
and hence
vf^{2}(v)\geq 36
and
vf(v)\geq 36
, by Remark
23
. Then if
u>1
one would have

u\geq 3
and
uf(u)\geq 9
and (
117
) would give

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<4\left(\frac{1}{9}+\frac{1}{36}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{36-16}\right)=1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
u=1
. Then (
117
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<4\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{36}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{36-16}\right)=\frac{37}{5},

and hence
w^{\prime}=1
or
2
. Now (
114
) gives

(118)

f(1+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)-16)=4(z^{\prime\prime 2}+f(v)).

By Remark
23
, if
vf^{2}(v)\geq 36
, then either
v=36
or
vf(v)\geq 64
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 64
.
If
v=36
, then (
116
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{4}{37}\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{36}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{36-16}\right)=\frac{1}{5}.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
vf(v)\geq 64
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 64
.
Now if
vf^{2}(v)=64
, then (
116
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{4}{65}\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{64}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{64-16}\right)=\frac{1}{12}.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So by Remark
23
,
vf(v)\geq 100
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 100
.
Notice that
f(v)=1,3
or
f(v)\geq 5
. If
f(v)\geq 5
, then (
117
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<4\left(\frac{1}{5}+\frac{1}{100}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{100-16}\right)=1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(v)=1
or
3
. If
f(v)=3
, then (
118
) gives

f(1+v)w^{\prime 2}(9v-16)=4z^{\prime\prime 2}+12
, with
w^{\prime}=1
or
2
, so modulo 3,
-f(1+v)\equiv z^{\prime\prime 2}
. But if
f(v)=3
, then
v+1\equiv 1\pmod{3}
. Hence, since
v+1=f(v+1)s^{2}(v+1)
, we have
f(v+1)\equiv 1\pmod{3}
. But then
-1\equiv z^{\prime\prime 2}\pmod{3}
, which is impossible. So
f(v)=1
and hence
v
is an even square,
v=4n^{2}
say. Notice that as
vf^{2}(v)\geq 100
and
f(v)=1
, we have
n\geq 5
. Equation (
118
) gives
f(1+4n^{2})w^{\prime 2}(4n^{2}-16)=4z^{\prime\prime 2}+4
, hence

f(1+4n^{2})w^{\prime 2}(n^{2}-4)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+1.

This is impossible modulo 4 if
w^{\prime}=2
, so
w^{\prime}=1
. So

(119)

f(1+4n^{2})(n^{2}-4)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+1.

Note that
f(1+4n^{2})\not=1
since otherwise
1+4n^{2}
would be a square, which is impossible. So, as the prime divisors of
1^{2}+(2n)^{2}
are all congruent to 1 modulo 4, we have
f(1+4n^{2})\geq 5
.
Now (
113
) gives
4n^{2}>z^{\prime\prime 2}
. Hence (
119
) gives

5(n^{2}-4)\leq f(1+4n^{2})(n^{2}-4)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+1<4n^{2}+1.

Thus
n^{2}<21
, but this is impossible as
n\geq 5
. Hence
y^{\prime\prime}=1
is not possible.
∎

Lemma 18
.

If
u=1
, then
v=4
.

Proof.

Suppose
u=1
and
v>4
. So
vf(v)\geq 16
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 16
, by Remark
23
. From Lemma
17
,
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
.
Then (
117
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<4\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{16}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{16\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{17}{3},

so
w^{\prime}=1
or
2
.
Note that if
f(v)\geq 5
, then
v\geq 20,vf(v)\geq 100
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 500
, so (
117
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<4\left(\frac{1}{5}+\frac{1}{100}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{500\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{105}{124}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(v)=1
or
3
. First suppose that
f(v)=3
. Then
v\geq 12,vf(v)\geq 36
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 108
, so (
117
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<4\left(\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{36}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{108\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{3}{2},

so
w^{\prime}=1
. As
f(v)=3
,
v
has the form
12n^{2}
, for some
n
.
Then (
114
) gives
f(1+12n^{2})(108n^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4z^{\prime\prime 2}+12y^{\prime\prime 2}
, so

f(1+12n^{2})(27n^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-4)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+3y^{\prime\prime 2}.

But then modulo 3, since
f(1+12n^{2})\equiv 1
, we have
-1\equiv z^{\prime\prime 2}
, which is impossible. So
f(v)=1
. In this case,

v
is an even square; i.e., it has the form
4n^{2}
, for some
n
.
Then (
114
) gives
f(1+4n^{2})w^{\prime 2}(4n^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4z^{\prime\prime 2}+4y^{\prime\prime 2}
, so

(120)

f(1+4n^{2})w^{\prime 2}(n^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-4)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2},

where from above,
w^{\prime}=1
or
2
. First suppose that
w^{\prime}=2
. Then modulo 4 we have
0\equiv z^{\prime\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2}
, so
y^{\prime\prime}
and
z^{\prime\prime}
are both even, say
y^{\prime\prime}=2y^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
z^{\prime\prime}=2z^{\prime\prime\prime}
. So we have

f(1+4n^{2})(4n^{2}y^{\prime\prime\prime 2}-4)=z^{\prime\prime\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime\prime 2}
, which modulo 4 gives
0\equiv z^{\prime\prime\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime\prime 2}
. So
y^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
z^{\prime\prime\prime}
are both even, say
y^{\prime\prime\prime}=2y_{4}
and
z^{\prime\prime\prime}=2z_{4}
. So we have

f(1+4n^{2})(4n^{2}y_{4}^{2}-1)=z_{4}^{2}+y_{4}^{2}.

But now, arguing modulo 4 again, we have
-1\equiv z_{4}^{2}+y_{4}^{2}
, which is impossible. Hence
w^{\prime}=1
. Thus (
120
) gives

(121)

f(1+4n^{2})(n^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-4)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2}.

Note that we can write
1+4n^{2}=f(1+4n^{2})m^{2}
, for
m:=s(1+4n^{2})
. Notice also from (
113
) and (
121
),

f(1+4n^{2})(n^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-4)<(4n^{2}+1)y^{\prime\prime 2}
,
so

n^{2}-\frac{4}{y^{\prime\prime 2}}<\frac{4n^{2}+1}{f(1+4n^{2})}=m^{2}.

Thus, as
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
, we have
n^{2}-1\leq n^{2}-\frac{4}{y^{\prime\prime 2}}<m^{2}
, so
n^{2}\leq m^{2}
, so
n\leq m
. Then we have

f(1+4n^{2})=\frac{1+4n^{2}}{m^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{m^{2}}+4.

If
m=1
then
n=1
and so
v{\not>}4
a contradiction. So
f(1+4n^{2})<5
. Thus, as
f(1+4n^{2})
is odd,
f(1+4n^{2})=3
. But this would imply that
1+4n^{2}
is divisible by
3
and hence
1+n^{2}\equiv 0\pmod{3}
, which is impossible.
∎

From Lemma
16
, either
u=1,v=4
or
y^{\prime\prime}=1
. We saw in Lemma

that
y^{\prime\prime}\not=1
, and in Lemma

that if

, then

.
So it remains to consider the situation where
v=4
. Assume for the moment that
y^{\prime\prime}=2
. From (

, which gives

,

.
From (

. Hence
f(u)=3
and again (

.
But then substituting

in (

) gives

which is impossible.
Hence
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 3
.

If

f(u)\geq 3
, (
117
) would give

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<4\left(\frac{1}{9}+\frac{1}{4\cdot 3}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{4\cdot 27\cdot 3^{3}-4}\right)=\frac{21}{23}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(u)=1
. Then (

) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<4\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{4\cdot 9-16}\right)=9,

so
w^{\prime}=1
or
2
.
As
f(u)=1
,
u
is a square. First suppose
u>1
. So
u\geq 9
. Since
u
is a square,

cannot be a square and thus
f(u+4)\geq 3
. Then (

) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{4}{3}\left(\frac{1}{9}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{4\cdot 3^{3}-4}\right)=\frac{13}{15}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Hence
u=1
.
Now (
114
) gives

5w^{\prime 2}(4y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4z^{\prime\prime 2}+4y^{\prime\prime 2}
, so

.
If
w^{\prime}=2

19y^{\prime\prime 2}=z^{\prime\prime 2}+80
. But modulo 19 this gives

, which is impossible.
So

and we have

and so

and

. This is the required solution:

\Sigma=2f(u+v)f(u)f(v)vw^{\prime 2}=40
and
\Sigma^{\prime}=u\Sigma/v=10
.

Case 2. Assume
u
is odd,
v
is even and the 2-adic order of
v
is odd.

We will show that in this case,
(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})=(24,12)
is the only possibility.

As
x
is an integer,
from (

) we can write
\Sigma=2f(u+v)f(u)w^{2}
, for some

.

, and as the 2-adic order of

f(v)s(v)

and

) gives

Thus

, so

, say

So (

and hence

(123)

4s(v)y^{\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime},

and (

) gives

Hence

From this last equation, notice that as

f(v)s(v)y^{\prime\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime\prime}\quad\text{and so}\quad vf(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}>uf(u)z^{\prime\prime 2},

and

(126)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-64)=16(f(u)z^{\prime\prime 2}+f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}).

Note that from the left-hand side of (

), we have

Furthermore, (

) and (

) give

Hence

(128)

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{f(u+v)}\left(\frac{1}{uf(u)f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)f(u)}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-64}\right)

and consequently

(129)

w^{\prime 2}<16\left(\frac{1}{uf(u)f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)f(u)}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-64}\right).

Lemma 19
.

f(v)=2
.

Proof.

Assume that
f(v)>2
. So
f(v)\geq 6,vf(v)\geq 36,vf^{2}(v)\geq 216
.
We first show that
f(u)=1
. Indeed, if
f(u)>1
, then
f(u)\geq 3,uf(u)\geq 9
and for all
y^{\prime\prime}
,
(
129
) would give

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{9\cdot 6}+\frac{1}{36\cdot 3}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{216\cdot 3-64}\right)=\frac{36}{73}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(u)=1
. Hence
u
is a square. Moreover, (
126
) gives

(130)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}-64)=16(z^{\prime\prime 2}+f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}).

Since
f(v)
is even and square-free, the five smallest possible values of
f(v)
are
2,6,10,14,22
. If
f(v)>14
, then
f(v)\geq 22
, so
v\geq 22
and for all
u,y^{\prime\prime}
,
(
129
) would give

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{22}+\frac{1}{22^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{22^{3}-64}\right)=\frac{1012}{1323}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(v)\leq 14
. Now suppose
f(v)=14
. Here
v\geq 14
and (
129
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{14}+\frac{1}{14^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{14^{3}-64}\right)=\frac{84}{67}<2,

so
w^{\prime}=1
. Furthermore,
v
is divisible by
7
, so as
\gcd(u,v)=1
and
u
is a square,
u+v\equiv 1,2
or
4\pmod{7}
, and thus

f(u+v)\equiv 1,2
or
4\pmod{7}
. Modulo 7, (
130
) gives

-f(u+v)\equiv 2z^{\prime\prime 2}
,
so
z^{\prime\prime 2}\equiv 3,6,5
respectively, but these congruences have no solutions. So
f(v)\not=14
.

Now suppose
f(v)=10
. Here (
129
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{10}+\frac{1}{10^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{10^{3}-64}\right)=\frac{220}{117}<2,

so
w^{\prime}=1
.
Then (
130
) gives
f(u+v)(100vy^{\prime\prime 2}-64)=16(z^{\prime\prime 2}+10y^{\prime\prime 2})
.
We have
v=10m^{2}
, for some
m
. So

(131)

f(u+10m^{2})(125(my^{\prime\prime})^{2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+10y^{\prime\prime 2}).

As
f(u)=1
, we have
u=n^{2}
, for some odd
n
. First suppose
f(n^{2}+10m^{2})=1
. So
n^{2}+10m^{2}=r^{2}
, for some odd
r
. Then

1+2m^{2}\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, and hence
m
must be even, say
m=2m^{\prime}
. So (
131
) gives

250(m^{\prime}y^{\prime\prime})^{2}-4=z^{\prime\prime 2}+10y^{\prime\prime 2}
and hence
z^{\prime\prime}
is even, say
z^{\prime\prime}=2z^{\prime\prime\prime}
. So
125(m^{\prime}y^{\prime\prime})^{2}-2=2z^{\prime\prime\prime 2}+5y^{\prime\prime 2}
. But one readily verifies that modulo 16, this equation
has no solution for
m^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime},z^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
Thus
f(n^{2}+10m^{2})\geq 3
.
Then (
128
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{3}\left(\frac{1}{10}+\frac{1}{10^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{10^{3}-64}\right)=\frac{220}{351}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(v)\not=10
.

Now suppose
f(v)=6
. Here (
129
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{6^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{6^{3}-64}\right)=\frac{84}{19}<5,

so

or
2
. Furthermore,
v
is divisible by

, so as

and

, and thus

. Hence modulo 3, (

) gives

,
which is impossible. So

.
∎

.

. So

for some

. In particular,

. Furthermore, here are some obvious useful facts:

, while

only for

and

.

, while

only for

and

.

, then

and

. Furthermore, if

, then

.

and

, then

.
