Lemma 20
.

Either
u=1
or
v=2
or
y^{\prime\prime}=1
.

Proof.

Suppose
u>1
and
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
. We will show that
v=2
. Let us assume for the moment that
v>98
, so
vf(v)\geq 256,vf^{2}(v)\geq 512
by Remark
24
(b). We also have
uf(u)\geq 9
by Remark
24
(c). Then using
f(u)\geq 1
, (
129
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{9\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{256}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{512\cdot 4-64}\right)=\frac{274}{279}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
v\leq 98
, and thus, as mentioned in the above remark,
v=2,8,18,32,50,72
or
98
. Our goal is to exclude the last 6 of these 7 possible
v
-values. We will first consider the cases
u=3
and
u=9
. Note that of our 6
v
-values of interest, we need only consider the ones relatively prime to
3
; that is, 8,32,50,98. For these four
v
-values, if
u=3
, then
\frac{16}{f(3+v)}(\frac{1}{9f(v)}+\frac{1}{3vf(v)})(1+\frac{64}{3vf^{2}(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}-64})
takes the respective values

\frac{2}{15},\frac{2}{69},\frac{4}{219},\frac{4}{435}
,
and as these values are all less than 1, we obtain a contradiction from (
128
). Similarly, if
u=9
, then
\frac{16}{f(9+v)}(\frac{1}{9f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)})(1+\frac{64}{vf^{2}(v)2^{2}-64})
takes the respective values

\frac{2}{9},\frac{2}{63},\frac{4}{207},\frac{4}{423}
,
and as these values are also all less than 1, we again obtain a contradiction from (
128
).
So we may assume that
u>3
and
u\not=9
. Then by Remark
24
(c), we have
uf(u)\geq 25
and for the five
v
-values
v=18,32,50,72,98
respectively, one finds that
16(\frac{1}{25f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)})(1+\frac{64}{vf^{2}(v)2^{2}-64})
takes the values

\frac{172}{175},\frac{114}{175},\frac{12}{23},\frac{194}{425},\frac{492}{1175}
.
As these values are all less than 1, we obtain a contradiction from (
129
). It remains to treat the case
v=8
, with
u>3
and
u\not=9
.
First note that in this case,
uf(u)\geq 25
by Remark
24
(c), and if
f(u)>1
, then
f(u)\geq 3
. But then (
129
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<16\left(\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{16\cdot 3}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{32\cdot 3\cdot 4-64}\right)=\frac{98}{125}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So we may assume
f(u)=1
, i.e.,
u
is a square. But then, as
u>1
,
u+8
is not a square and so
f(u+8)\geq 3
. Then (
128
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{3}\left(\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{16}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{32\cdot 4-64}\right)=\frac{22}{25}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Hence
v=8
is impossible. Thus
v=2
.
∎

Lemma 21
.

If
y^{\prime\prime}=1
, then
u=1
.

Proof.

Suppose
y^{\prime\prime}=1
, and arguing by contradiction, suppose
u>1
. Note that (
125
) gives
vf(v)>uf(u)z^{\prime\prime 2}\geq uf(u)
. Also, by (
127
),
vf^{2}(v)f(u)>64
. So if
v=2
, then
vf^{2}(v)=8
and hence
f(u)>8
, contradicting the fact that
vf(v)>uf(u)
. Hence
v>2
.
By Lemma
19
,
f(v)=2
. So, as
v>2
, we have
v=2m^{2}
for some
m\geq 2
.
Notice also that if
m=2
, then
vf^{2}(v)=32
, and so
vf^{2}(v)f(u)>64
gives
f(u)>1
.

If
m\geq 11
, then as
uf(u)\geq 9
by Remark
24
(c), (
129
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{9\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4m^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{8m^{2}-64}\right)=\frac{1004}{1017}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So we need only consider
2\leq m\leq 10
. First consider
u=3
and
u=9
. The numbers
v=2m^{2}
with
2\leq m\leq 10
and
\gcd(u,v)=1
are given by
m=2,4,5,7,8,10
.
For
u=3
and
m=2,4,5,7,8,10
, the values of
\frac{16}{f(3+2m^{2})}(\frac{1}{9\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{3\cdot 4m^{2}})(1+\frac{64}{8m^{2}\cdot 3-64})
are respectively

\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{30},\frac{4}{201},\frac{4}{417},\frac{1}{138},\frac{1}{219}.

As these values are all less than 1, we obtain a contradiction from (
128
).
Now let
u=9
. Here
f(u)=1
and so, as we observed at the beginning of this proof,
m>2
. For
m=4,5,7,8,10
, the values of
\frac{16}{f(9+2m^{2})}(\frac{1}{9\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4m^{2}})(1+\frac{64}{8m^{2}-64})
are respectively

\frac{1}{18},\frac{4}{153},\frac{4}{369},\frac{1}{126},\frac{1}{207}.

As these values are all less than 1, we again obtain a contradiction from (
128
).

From what we have just shown, we may suppose that
u\geq 5
and
u\not=9
, so
uf(u)\geq 25
, by Remark
24
(c). If
m\geq 5
, then (
129
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4\cdot 25}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{8\cdot 25-64}\right)=\frac{12}{17}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

It remains to treat the cases
m=2,3,4
for
u\geq 5
and
u\not=9
.

If
u=5
, then for
m=2,3,4
, the values of
\frac{16}{f(5+2m^{2})}(\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{5\cdot 4m^{2}})(1+\frac{64}{8m^{2}\cdot 5-64})
are respectively

\frac{1}{15},\frac{4}{185},\frac{1}{90}
, which is impossible by (
128
).
Similarly, if
u=25
, then for
m=3,4
, the values of
\frac{16}{f(25+2m^{2})}(\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4m^{2}})(1+\frac{64}{8m^{2}-64})
are respectively

\frac{4}{25},\frac{1}{50}
, which is again impossible by (
128
). For
m=2
we don’t need to consider
u=25
as
f(25)=1
and as we observed at the beginning of this proof,
f(u)>1
for
m=2
. Thus for

m=2,3,4
, we may assume that
u>5
and
u\not=25
.

For
m=4
with
u>5
and
u\not=25
, we have
uf(u)\geq 49
by Remark
24
(c), so (
129
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<16\left(\frac{1}{49\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{64}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{64\cdot 2-64}\right)=\frac{81}{98}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

It therefore remains to treat the cases
m=2,3
for
u\geq 7
and
u\not=25
. First let
m=2
. Then, as we saw at the beginning of the proof,
f(u)\geq 3
. If
f(u)\geq 5
, then as
uf(u)\geq 49
, (
129
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<16\left(\frac{1}{49\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{16\cdot 5}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{32\cdot 5-64}\right)=\frac{89}{147}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

If
f(u)=3
, then
u
is divisible by 3, so
u+v=u+8\equiv 2\pmod{3}
, and hence
u+v
is not a square, and neither is it divisible by
3
. So
f(u+v)\not=1
, and consequently
f(u+v)\geq 5
. Thus, using
uf(u)\geq 49
and
f(u)=3
, (
128
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{5}\left(\frac{1}{49\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{16\cdot 3}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{32\cdot 3-64}\right)=\frac{73}{245}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So the case
m=2
is also impossible.

Finally let
m=3
. If
f(u)\geq 3
, then as
uf(u)\geq 49
, (
129
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<16\left(\frac{1}{49\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{36\cdot 3}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{72\cdot 3-64}\right)=\frac{412}{931}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

If
f(u)=1
, then
u
is a square, so
u+v=u+18
is not a square. Thus
f(u+v)\not=1
. Furthermore,
u
is not divisible by
3
since
v=18
and
\gcd(u,v)=1
. So
u+18
is not divisible by
3
. Hence
f(u+v)\geq 5
. Furthermore, as
uf(u)\geq 49
and
f(u)=1
, we have
u\geq 49
.
Thus (
128
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{5}\left(\frac{1}{u\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{36}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{72-64}\right)=\frac{4(u+18)}{5u}.

As
w^{\prime}\geq 1
, we obtain
u<72
, and so as
u
is an odd square with
u\geq 49
, we have
u=49
. But then
f(u+v)=57
and
(
128
) gives a contradiction, as

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{67}\left(\frac{1}{49\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{36}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{72-64}\right)=\frac{4}{49}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 22
.

y^{\prime\prime}\not=1
.

Proof.

Suppose
y^{\prime\prime}=1
, so by the above lemma,
u=1
. In this case, (
126
) gives

(132)

f(1+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)-64)=16(z^{\prime\prime 2}+f(v)).

From Lemma
19
,
f(v)=2
, so
v
has the form
v=2m^{2}
for some
m
. Then (
132
) gives

f(1+v)w^{\prime 2}(8m^{2}-64)=16z^{\prime\prime 2}+32
, so

(133)

f(1+2m^{2})w^{\prime 2}(m^{2}-8)=2z^{\prime\prime 2}+4.

Notice that the 2-adic order is exactly 2, since on the right hand side it is 1 or 2 and on the left hand side at least 2.
So
z^{\prime\prime}
is even, say
z^{\prime\prime}=2z^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Suppose that
m
is even and write
m=2m^{\prime}
. After replacing and dividing by 4 one gets
f(1+8m^{\prime 2})w^{\prime 2}(m^{\prime 2}-2)=2z^{\prime\prime\prime 2}+1
, so
w^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime}
are both odd. Observed modulo 8 this gives a contradiction, since the LHS is
-1
and the RHS is 1 or 3 modulo 8. Hence
m
must be odd and thus from (
133
)
w^{\prime}
is necessarily even, say
w^{\prime}=2w^{\prime\prime}
, giving

(134)

f(1+2m^{2})w^{\prime\prime 2}(m^{2}-8)=2z^{\prime\prime\prime 2}+1.

From (
125
) we have
m>z^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Thus
(
134
) gives

(135)

w^{\prime\prime 2}<\frac{2m^{2}+1}{f(1+2m^{2})(m^{2}-8)}.

Notice that from (
134
) we have
m^{2}>8
, so
m\geq 3
and is odd.
If
m=3
, then (
135
)
gives

w^{\prime\prime 2}<\frac{19}{19}=1
, a contradiction. If
f(1+2m^{2})=3
then
\frac{2m^{2}+1}{3(m^{2}-8)}\leq 1
for
m\geq 5
, absurd. Finally, if
f(1+2m^{2})=1
then
w^{\prime\prime 2}<\frac{2m^{2}+1}{m^{2}-8}\leq 3
for
m\geq 5
and so
w^{\prime\prime}=1
. Replacing in (
134
) gives
m^{2}=2z^{\prime\prime 2}+9
, which modulo 3 implies either
1\equiv 0
or
1\equiv 2
.
∎

Lemma 23
.

If
v=2
, then
u=1
.

Proof.

Suppose that
v=2
and
u>1
. So by Remark
24
(c),
uf(u)\geq 9
.
From the previous lemma,
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
. Assume for the moment that
y^{\prime\prime}=2
. By (
127
),
vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}>64
, which gives
f(u)>2
, so
f(u)\geq 3
.
Now from (
125
), we have
uf(u)z^{\prime\prime 2}<vf(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}=16
. So
uf(u)\geq 9
gives
z^{\prime}=1
and then
uf(u)<16
and
f(u)\geq 3
give
u=3
.
Then
f(u+v)=5
and so (
128
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{5}\left(\frac{1}{9\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4\cdot 3}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{8\cdot 3\cdot 4-64}\right)=\frac{4}{3}<2,

so
w^{\prime}=1
. But then,
by (
126
),
5(8\cdot 3\cdot 4-64)=16(3z^{\prime\prime 2}+8)
, which has no integer solution for
z^{\prime\prime}
. So
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 3
.

Note that if
f(u)\geq 7
, then
u\geq 7
and by (
129
)

w^{\prime 2}<16\left(\frac{1}{49\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4\cdot 7}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{8\cdot 7\cdot 9-64}\right)=\frac{324}{385}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So it suffices to deal with the three cases
f(u)=1,3,5
. First suppose
f(u)=5
. So
uf(u)\geq 25
. As
f(u)=5
and
v=2
, we have
u+v\equiv 2\pmod{5}
and hence
u+v
is not a square. So
f(u+v)\geq 3
. Hence by (
128
)

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{3}\left(\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4\cdot 5}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{8\cdot 5\cdot 9-64}\right)=\frac{84}{185}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(u)\not=5
. Now suppose
f(u)=3
. So
uf(u)\geq 9
. We have
u+v\equiv 2\pmod{3}
and hence
u+v
is not a square. So
f(u+v)\geq 3
. But
f(u+v)\not=3
, because
u+v\equiv 2\pmod{3}
. So
f(u+v)\geq 5
. Hence by (
128
)

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{5}\left(\frac{1}{9\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4\cdot 3}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{8\cdot 3\cdot 9-64}\right)=\frac{12}{19}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(u)\not=3
. Finally, suppose
f(u)=1
. Then
(
126
) gives

(136)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2}).

As
f(u)=1
, so
u
is an odd square, say
u=n^{2}
. Thus, as
u>1
by hypothesis,
u\geq 9
.
As
u
is a square,
u+2
is not a square, so
f(u+v)\geq 3
. By (
128
),

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{3}\left(\frac{1}{9\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{8\cdot 9-64}\right)=\frac{44}{3}<15.

So
w^{\prime}=1,2
or
3
. Suppose for the moment that
y^{\prime\prime}=3
. Then as
f(u+v)
is odd,
w^{\prime}
must be even, by (
136
), so
w^{\prime}=2
. Then (
136
) gives

2f(u+v)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+18
.
It follows that
z^{\prime\prime}
must be even and hence
f(u+v)\geq 11
. But then (
128
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{11}\left(\frac{1}{9\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{8\cdot 9-64}\right)=4,

contradicting
w^{\prime}=2
. Hence
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 4
.

Note that for
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 4
, if
f(u+v)\geq 11
, then (
128
) would give

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{11}\left(\frac{1}{9\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{8\cdot 16-64}\right)=\frac{8}{9}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So, as
f(u+v)
is square-free,
f(u+v)=3,5
or
7
. But then
u+v
would be divisible by
3,5,7
respectively. Since there is no
n
for which
n^{2}+2\equiv 0
modulo 5 or 7, we conclude that
f(u+v)=3
. Thus
n^{2}+2=u+v
is divisible by 3 and hence
n^{2}\equiv 1\pmod{3}
. Thus, for
u>1
we have
n>3
and thus
u\geq 25
.
Then (
128
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{3}\left(\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{8\cdot 16-64}\right)=\frac{72}{25}<3.

So
w^{\prime}=1
. But then
(
136
) would give
3(y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2})
, so
0=24+2z^{\prime\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2}
, which is obviously impossible. Hence
u=1
.
∎

Lemma 24
.

If
u=1
, then
v=2
.

Proof.

Suppose that
u=1
and
v>2
. So by Remark
24
,
v\geq 8,vf(v)\geq 16
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 32
. By Lemma
22
,
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
.
Then (
129
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{16}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{32\cdot 4-64}\right)=18,

so
w^{\prime}=1,2,3
or
4
.
Lemma
19
and Equation (
126
) give

(137)

f(1+v)w^{\prime 2}(vy^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2}),

while, setting
v=2m^{2}
, (
125
) gives

(138)

(2m)^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}>z^{\prime\prime 2}.

Let us first dispense with the case
v=8
. Suppose
v=8
. Then
f(1+v)=1
and substituting in (
137
), the four possibilities for
w^{\prime}
give:

(a)

w^{\prime}=1
:
2(y^{\prime\prime 2}-2)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2}
, so
-4=z^{\prime\prime 2}
, which is obviously impossible.

(b)

w^{\prime}=2
:
8(y^{\prime\prime 2}-2)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2}
, so
6y^{\prime\prime 2}-16=z^{\prime\prime 2}
, which is impossible modulo 3.

(c)

w^{\prime}=3
:
18(y^{\prime\prime 2}-2)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2}
, so
(4y)^{\prime\prime 2}=z^{\prime\prime 2}+6^{2}
, which is impossible as there is no such Pythagorean triple.

(d)

w^{\prime}=4
:
32(y^{\prime\prime 2}-2)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2}
, so
30y^{\prime\prime 2}-64=z^{\prime\prime 2}
, which is impossible modulo 3.

So
v>8
and hence
v\geq 18,vf(v)\geq 36
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 72
.
Then (
129
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{36}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{72\cdot 4-64}\right)=\frac{76}{7}<11,

so
w^{\prime}=1,2
or
3
. Substituting
w^{\prime}=3
in (
137
) for
v=18
and
32
gives respectively

19\cdot 9(9y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2})\ \text{and}\ 33\cdot 9(8y^{\prime\prime 2}-4)=z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2}.

However, neither of these equations has a solution modulo 64. So
w^{\prime}=1
or
2
for
v=18
and
32
. For
v>32
we have
v\geq 50
and
(
129
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{100}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{200\cdot 4-64}\right)=\frac{204}{23}<9,

so
w^{\prime}=1
or
2
. Thus we have
w^{\prime}=1
or
2
for all
v\geq 18
.

First suppose
w^{\prime}=2
. Note that if
f(1+2m^{2})\geq 3
, then (
138
) and (
137
) give

12(m^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)\leq 4f(1+2m^{2})(m^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2})<(8m^{2}+4)y^{\prime\prime 2},

so
(m^{2}-1)y^{\prime\prime 2}<24
. But for
v\geq 18
, we have
m\geq 3
. So
(m^{2}-1)y^{\prime\prime 2}<24
gives
y^{\prime\prime 2}<3
, hence
y^{\prime\prime}=1
, contrary to Lemma
22
. We conclude that
f(1+2m^{2})=1
. Thus (
137
) gives
4(m^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2})
, so

2(m^{2}-1)y^{\prime\prime 2}=z^{\prime\prime 2}+16.

Since
f(1+2m^{2})=1
, we have that
1+2m^{2}
is a square, say
1+2m^{2}=n^{2}
. But investigations show that the simultaneous equations
2(m^{2}-1)y^{\prime\prime 2}=z^{\prime\prime 2}+16
and
1+2m^{2}=n^{2}
have no integer solution modulo 128. Hence
w^{\prime}=2
is impossible.

Finally, suppose
w^{\prime}=1
. Note that if
f(1+2m^{2})\geq 11
, then (
138
) and (
137
) give:

11(m^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)\leq f(1+2m^{2})(m^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2})<(8m^{2}+4)y^{\prime\prime 2},

so
(3m^{2}-4)y^{\prime\prime 2}<88
. But for
v\geq 18
, we have
m\geq 3
and so
(3m^{2}-4)y^{\prime\prime 2}<88
gives
y^{\prime\prime 2}<88/23<4
, hence
y^{\prime\prime}=1
, contrary to Lemma
22
. So
f(1+2m^{2})=1,3,5
or
7
.

For
f(1+2m^{2})=7
, (
137
) gives
7(m^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2})
, which has no solution modulo
49
. So
f(1+2m^{2})\not=7
.

For
f(1+2m^{2})=5
, (
137
) gives
5(m^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2})
, which has no solution modulo
25
. So
f(1+2m^{2})\not=5
.

For
f(1+2m^{2})=1
and
3
, the calculation is slightly more complicated. For
f(1+2m^{2})=1

we consider the pair of the simultaneous equations
m^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-8=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2})
and
1+2m^{2}=n^{2}
, while for
f(1+2m^{2})=3

we consider the pair of the simultaneous equations
3(m^{2}y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2})
and
1+2m^{2}=3n^{2}
. In both cases one finds that the pair of equations has no solution modulo 64. Thus
w^{\prime}=1
is also impossible.
∎

Given the above lemmas, it remains to treat the case where
u=1,v=2
and
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
. By (
127
),
vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-64>0
, so
y^{\prime\prime 2}>8
. Thus
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 3
. Then (
128
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{3}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\left(1+\frac{64}{8\cdot 9-64}\right)=36,

so
w^{\prime}\leq 5
. Equation (
126
) gives

(139)

3w^{\prime 2}(y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2}),

One finds that for
w^{\prime}=1,3
and
5
, (
139
) has no solution modulo 64. So
w^{\prime}=2
or
4
.

For
w^{\prime}=4
, (
125
) and (
139
) give

48(y^{\prime\prime 2}-8)=2(z^{\prime\prime 2}+2y^{\prime\prime 2})<12y^{\prime\prime 2}
,
so
3y^{\prime\prime 2}<32
. Thus, as
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 3
, we have
y^{\prime\prime}=3
, and (
139
) gives
z^{\prime\prime 2}=6
, which is obviously impossible. So
w^{\prime}=2
.

Finally, for
w^{\prime}=2
, Equation (
139
) has a unique positive integer solution:
y^{\prime\prime}=z^{\prime\prime}=4
.
From the definitions, for
u=1,v=2
, we have

\Sigma=2f(u+v)f(u)vf(v)w^{\prime 2}/4=6w^{\prime 2}=24
. Consequently,
\Sigma^{\prime}=u\Sigma/v=12
. This is the required case 2 solution.

Case 3. Assume
u
is even,
v
is odd, and the 2-adic order of
u
is even.

We will show that in this case,
(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})=(10,40)
and
(18,32)
are the only two possibilities.

As
x
is an integer,
from (
109
) we can write
\Sigma=2f(u+v)f(u)w^{2}
.
Note
v
divides
\Sigma
, so
v
divides
w^{2}
, and hence
f(v)s(v)
divides
w
. Thus, setting
w=f(v)s(v)w^{\prime}
we may write
\Sigma=2f(u+v)f(u)f(v)vw^{\prime 2}
. Then
(
108
) gives

(140)

f(u+v)f(u)f(v)vw^{\prime 2}(vf(u)y^{\prime 2}-16)=4(z^{2}+vf(u)y^{\prime 2}).

Thus
vf(u)
divides
16z^{2}
, so
f(v)s(v)f(u)
divides
z
, say
z=f(v)s(v)f(u)z^{\prime}
. So (
107
) gives
vy^{\prime}>s(u)f(v)s(v)f(u)z^{\prime}
and hence

(141)

s(v)y^{\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime},

and (
140
) gives

(142)

f(u+v)f(v)w^{\prime 2}(vf(u)y^{\prime 2}-16)=4(f(v)f(u)z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime 2}).

Hence
f(v)
divides
y^{\prime}
. Let
y^{\prime}=f(v)y^{\prime\prime}
.
Then (
141
) gives

(143)

f(v)s(v)y^{\prime\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime}\quad\text{and so}\quad vf(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}>uf(u)z^{\prime 2},

and (
142
) gives

(144)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4(f(u)z^{\prime 2}+f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}).

Remark 25
.

Note that
v,f(v),f(u)
and
f(u+v)
are all odd. It follows from (
144
) that
w^{\prime}y^{\prime\prime}
is even.

Note that from the left-hand side of (
144
), we have

(145)

vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}>16.

Furthermore, (
143
) and (
144
) give

uf(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)<4(v+u)f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}.

Hence

(146)

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{4}{f(u+v)}\left(\frac{1}{uf(u)f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)f(u)}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16}\right)

and consequently

(147)

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{uf(u)f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)f(u)}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16}\right).

Remark 26
.

Note that as
u
is even and the 2-adic order of
u
is even, we have
u\geq 4
. The first twelve possible values of
u
are the same as the
v
values shown in Table
8
.

Lemma 25
.

The following conditions hold.

(a)

If
f(u)>1
, then
v=1
and
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 7
.

(b)

f(u)\leq 3
.

(c)

If
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
, then
f(v)=1
.

Proof.

(a). Suppose
f(u)>1
, so
f(u)\geq 3
,
u\geq 12
and
uf(u)\geq 36
. Suppose also that
v\geq 3
, so
vf(v)\geq 9
. Then for
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 1
, (
147
) would give

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{9\cdot 3}+\frac{1}{36}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{9\cdot 3-16}\right)=\frac{7}{11}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
v=1
. Then (
143
) gives

y^{\prime\prime 2}>uf(u)z^{\prime 2}\geq 36
, so
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 7
.

(b). If
f(u)>3
, then
f(u)\geq 5
,
u\geq 20
and
uf(u)\geq 100
. Then for
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 7
, (
147
) would give

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{20}+\frac{1}{5}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{5\cdot 7^{2}-16}\right)=\frac{1029}{1145}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

(c). Suppose
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
and
f(v)\geq 3
, so
vf(v)\geq 9
. For all
u\geq 4
, (
147
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{9}+\frac{1}{4\cdot 3}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{27\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{21}{23}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

∎

Lemma 26
.

f(u)=1
.

Proof.

Suppose
f(u)>1
. By Lemma
25
,
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 7,v=1,f(u)=3
and so
u\geq 12
.
Now (
147
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{3\cdot 12}+\frac{1}{3}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{3\cdot 7^{2}-16}\right)=\frac{637}{393}<2,

so
w^{\prime}=1
. Hence (
144
) gives

(148)

f(u+v)(3y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4(3z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2}).

Modulo 3 we have
y^{\prime\prime 2}\equiv-f(u+v)
. In particular,
f(u+v)=1
is impossible. So as
u+v
is odd and
\gcd(u,u+v)=1
, we have
f(u+v)\geq 5
. Then (
146
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{4}{5}\left(\frac{1}{3\cdot 12}+\frac{1}{3}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{3\cdot 7^{2}-16}\right)=\frac{637}{1965}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

∎

Lemma 27
.

The following conditions hold.

(a)

Either
u=4
or
u=16
.

(b)

If
u=4
, then
v=1
.

(c)

If
u=16
, then
v=9
.

Proof.

(a). By Lemma
26
,
u
is an even square, say
u=4n^{2}
. Suppose
n\geq 3
, so
u\geq 36
. First suppose that
y^{\prime\prime}=1
. Then (
143
) gives

vf(v)>uf(u)z^{\prime 2}\geq 36
.
Then (
147
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{36}+\frac{1}{36}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{36-16}\right)=\frac{2}{5}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Hence
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
and so by Lemma
25
(c),
f(v)=1
. Suppose for the moment that
v=1
. Then (
143
) gives
y^{\prime\prime 2}>uf(u)z^{\prime 2}\geq 36
. So
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 7
. Moreover,
u+v=4n^{2}+1
is not a square, and is not divisible by
3
. So
f(u+v)\geq 5
.
Then (
146
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{4}{5}\left(\frac{1}{36}+\frac{1}{1}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{7^{2}-16}\right)=\frac{1813}{1485}<2,

so
w^{\prime}=1
. Then (
146
) gives

f(u+v)<4\left(\frac{1}{36}+\frac{1}{1}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{7^{2}-16}\right)=\frac{1813}{297}<7,

so, as
f(u+v)
is odd and
f(u+v)\geq 5
, we have
f(u+v)=5
.
Then (
144
) gives

y^{\prime\prime 2}=4z^{\prime 2}+80
. But we saw above that (
143
) gives
y^{\prime\prime 2}>uf(u)z^{\prime 2}\geq 36z^{\prime 2}
. So we have

4z^{\prime 2}+80\geq 36z^{\prime 2}
and hence
2z^{\prime 2}<5
. Thus
z^{\prime 2}=1
. But then
y^{\prime\prime 2}=4z^{\prime 2}+80
has no integer solution for
y^{\prime\prime}
.
Consequently,
v=1
is not possible.

As
f(v)=1
, we now have
v\geq 9
. But then as
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
, (
147
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{36}+\frac{1}{9}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{9\cdot 4-16}\right)=1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

We conclude that
u=4n^{2}
with
n\leq 2
.

(b). Let
u=4
and assume
v>1
. First suppose that
y^{\prime\prime}=1
. Then (
143
) gives

vf(v)>uf(u)z^{\prime 2}\geq 4
. Hence
vf(v)\geq 9
.
Also, (
145
) gives
vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}>16
, so
vf^{2}(v)>16
. So
v\not=9
, and consequently either
f(v)\geq 3
or
v
is an odd square with
v\geq 25
. In either case,
vf^{2}(v)\geq 25
.

First suppose that
v
is an odd square with
v\geq 25
. Then
u+v=4+v
is not a square, so
f(u+v)>1
. Moreover, as
v
is a square
4+v\not\equiv 0\pmod{3}
, so
f(u+v)\not=3
. Hence
f(u+v)\geq 5
.
Then (
146
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{4}{5}\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{25}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25-16}\right)=\frac{29}{45}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Now suppose
f(v)\geq 3
. Then (
147
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{4\cdot 3}+\frac{1}{9}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{3^{3}-16}\right)=\frac{21}{11}<2.

So
w^{\prime}=1
. But as
y^{\prime\prime}=1
, this contradicts Remark
25
. We conclude that
y^{\prime\prime}=1
is not possible.

We now consider
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
. By Lemma
25
(c),
f(v)=1
. Suppose
v>1
.
Since
f(v)=1
,
v
is an odd square. So
4+v
is not a square, and hence
f(u+v)\geq 3
.
Then, as
v\geq 9
, (
146
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{4}{3}\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{9}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{9\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{13}{15}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Hence
v=1
, as required.

(c). Let
u=16
and assume
v\not=9
. First suppose that
y^{\prime\prime}=1
and that
f(v)=1
. Then (
143
) gives

vf(v)>uf(u)z^{\prime 2}\geq 16
. As
f(v)=1
,
v
is an odd square, it follows that
v\geq 25
.
Then (
147
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{16}+\frac{1}{25}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25-16}\right)=\frac{41}{36}<2,

so
w^{\prime}=1
. But as
y^{\prime\prime}=1
, this contradicts Remark
25
.

Now suppose that
y^{\prime\prime}=1
and that
f(v)>1
. So
f(v)\geq 3
.
If
v=3
, then (
147
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{16\cdot 3}+\frac{1}{9}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{27-16}\right)=\frac{57}{44}<2,

so
w^{\prime}=1
. Once again, this contradicts Remark
25
.

If
v>3
then we have
v\geq 5
and so for
f(v)\geq 3

(
147
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{16}+\frac{1}{15}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{45-16}\right)=\frac{93}{116}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

We conclude that
y^{\prime\prime}=1
is not possible.

We now consider
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
. By Lemma
25
(c),
f(v)=1
. So
v
is an odd square. Note that if
v\geq 25
, then
(
147
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{16}+\frac{1}{25}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{41}{84}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So it remains to eliminate the possibility that
v=1
.

Let
v=1
. Then (
143
) gives

y^{\prime\prime 2}>uf(u)z^{\prime 2}\geq 16
, so
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 5
. Also
f(u+v)=17
.
Then (
146
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{4}{17}\left(\frac{1}{16}+\frac{1}{1}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25-16}\right)=\frac{25}{36}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Hence
v=9
, as required.
∎

By the previous lemma, we have
u=4,v=1
or
u=16,v=9
. Consider the first case. Here (
145
) gives
y^{\prime\prime 2}>16
, so
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 5
. Then as
f(u+v)=5
, (
146
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{4}{5}\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{1}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25-16}\right)=\frac{25}{9}<3,

so
w^{\prime}=1
.
Equation (
144
) gives
5(y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4(z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2})
, so
y^{\prime\prime 2}=4z^{\prime 2}+80
, which has the solution
y^{\prime\prime}=12,z^{\prime}=4
.
From the definitions,
w=f(v)s(v)w^{\prime}=w^{\prime}
. Then
\Sigma=2f(u+v)f(u)w^{2}=10
, and
\Sigma^{\prime}=\frac{u}{v}\Sigma=4\Sigma=40
, which is one of the desired solutions.

Now consider the second case,
u=16,v=9
. Here (
145
) gives
9y^{\prime\prime 2}>16
, so
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
. Then (
147
) gives

w^{\prime 2}<4\left(\frac{1}{16}+\frac{1}{9}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{9\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{5}{4}<2,

so
w^{\prime}=1
. Equation (
144
) gives
(9y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4(z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2})
, so
5y^{\prime\prime 2}=4z^{\prime 2}+16
. This has infinitely many solutions.
From the definitions,
w=f(v)s(v)w^{\prime}=3w^{\prime}=3
.
Then
\Sigma=2f(u+v)f(u)w^{2}=18
, and
\Sigma^{\prime}=\frac{u}{v}\Sigma=16\Sigma/9=32
, which is the other desired Case 3 solution.

Case 4. Assume
u
is even,
v
is odd, and the 2-adic order of
u
is odd.

We will show that in this case,
(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})=(12,24)
is the only possibility.

As
x
is an integer,
from (
109
) we can write
2\Sigma=f(u+v)f(u)w^{2}
.
Note
v
divides
\Sigma
, so
v
divides
w^{2}
, and hence
f(v)s(v)
divides
w
. Thus, setting
w=f(v)s(v)w^{\prime}
we may write
2\Sigma=f(u+v)f(u)f(v)vw^{\prime 2}
. Then
(
108
) gives

(149)

f(u+v)f(u)f(v)vw^{\prime 2}(vf(u)y^{\prime 2}-16)=16(z^{2}+vf(u)y^{\prime 2}).

Thus
vf(u)
divides
16z^{2}
, so
f(v)s(v)\frac{f(u)}{2}
divides
z
, say
2z=f(v)s(v)f(u)z^{\prime}
. So (
107
) gives
2vy^{\prime}>s(u)f(v)s(v)f(u)z^{\prime}
and hence

(150)

2s(v)y^{\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime},

and (
149
) gives

(151)

f(u+v)f(v)w^{\prime 2}(vf(u)y^{\prime 2}-16)=4(f(v)f(u)z^{\prime 2}+4y^{\prime 2}).

Hence
f(v)
divides
y^{\prime}
. Let
y^{\prime}=f(v)y^{\prime\prime}
.
Then (
150
) gives

(152)

2f(v)s(v)y^{\prime\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime}\quad\text{and so}\quad 4vf(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}>uf(u)z^{\prime 2},

and (
151
) gives

(153)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4(f(u)z^{\prime 2}+4f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}).

Note that from the left-hand side of (
153
), we have

(154)

vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}>16.

Furthermore, (
152
) and (
153
) give

uf(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)<16(v+u)f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}.

Hence

(155)

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{f(v+u)}\left(\frac{1}{uf(u)f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)f(u)}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16}\right)

and consequently

(156)

w^{\prime 2}<16\left(\frac{1}{uf(u)f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)f(u)}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16}\right).

Lemma 28
.

The following conditions hold.

(a)

f(v)\leq 5
.

(b)

If
f(v)>1
, then
f(u)=2
.

(c)

If
f(v)=3
, then either
v=3
or
u=2
.

Proof.

(a). Suppose that
f(v)\geq 7
. So
vf(v)\geq 7^{2}
and

. Also, as the 2-adic order of
u
is odd, we have
f(u)\geq 2
, so
u\geq 2
and
uf(u)\geq 4
. Then for all
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 1
,
(
156
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{4\cdot 7}+\frac{1}{7^{2}\cdot 2}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{7^{3}\cdot 2-16}\right)=\frac{252}{335}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

(b). Suppose that
f(v)\geq 3
and
f(u)>2
. Then
f(u)\geq 6
, and so
uf(u)\geq 36
. And
vf(v)\geq 9
and
vf^{2}(v)\geq 27
. Then for all
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 1
,
(
156
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{36\cdot 3}+\frac{1}{9\cdot 6}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{27\cdot 6-16}\right)=\frac{36}{73}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

(c). Suppose that
f(v)=3
and that
v>3
and
u>2
. As
f(v)=3
,
v
has the form
v=3m^{2}
, for some odd
m
. By Part (b),
f(u)=2
, so
u
has the form
u=2n^{2}
, for some integer
n
. So
v\geq 27
and
u\geq 8
.
Then (
156
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{16\cdot 3}+\frac{1}{3^{4}\cdot 2}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{3^{5}\cdot 2-16}\right)=\frac{21}{47}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

∎

Lemma 29
.

f(v)=1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
28
(a),
f(v)\leq 5
. First suppose
f(v)=5
. By Lemma
28
(b),
f(u)=2
. If
u>2
, then
u\geq 8
and
uf(u)\geq 16
.
Then (
156
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{16\cdot 5}+\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{5^{3}\cdot 2-16}\right)=\frac{5}{9}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
u=2
. But
v
has the form
v=5m^{2}
, for some odd
m
, so
u+v=2+5m^{2}
, and this cannot be a square as
2+5m^{2}\equiv 3\pmod{4}
. Hence
f(u+v)\geq 3
. Then by (
155
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{3}\left(\frac{1}{4\cdot 5}+\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{5^{3}\cdot 2-16}\right)=\frac{140}{351}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(v)\not=5
.

Now suppose
f(v)=3
, so
v
has the form
v=3m^{2}
, for some odd
m
. By Lemma
28
(b),
f(u)=2
, so
u
has the form
u=2n^{2}
, for some integer
n
. By Lemma
28
(c), either
v=3
or
u=2
. We claim that in both cases,
u+v
is not a square. Indeed, if
v=3
, then
u+v=2n^{2}+3
isn’t a square since modulo 8,
2n^{2}+3
is either
3
or
5
, according to whether
n
is even or odd, but the quadratic residues modulo 8 are
0,1
and
4
. Similarly, if
u=2
, then
u+v=2+3m^{2}
isn’t a square as
2+3m^{2}\equiv 2\pmod{3}
.
Thus, in both cases,
f(u+v)>1
and so, as
\gcd(u+v,v)=1
, we have
f(u+v)\geq 5
. Then by (
155
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{5}\left(\frac{1}{4\cdot 3}+\frac{1}{2\cdot 3^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{3^{3}\cdot 2-16}\right)=\frac{12}{19}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

∎

Lemma 30
.

If
y^{\prime\prime}>1
, then
v=1
.

Proof.

By the previous lemma,
f(v)=1
, so
v
is an odd square, say
v=m^{2}
.
Suppose
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
and that
v\geq 9
. First suppose that
f(u)>2
. Then
f(u)\geq 6
and (
156
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{36}+\frac{1}{9\cdot 6}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{9\cdot 6\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{4}{5}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(u)=2
. Hence
u
has the form
u=2n^{2}
, for some integer
n
.

Now suppose for the moment that
v\geq 25
and that
u\geq 18
. Then
uf(u)\geq 36
and (
156
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{36}+\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25\cdot 2\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{172}{207}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So either
v=9
or
u<18
. First consider the case where
u<18
and
v\geq 25
. There are two possibilities: either
u=2
or
u=8
.
If
u=8
, then
u+v=8+m^{2}
cannot be a square for
m>1
. So
f(u+v)\geq 3
. Then
uf(u)=16
, and
(
155
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{3}\left(\frac{1}{16}+\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25\cdot 2\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{11}{23}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

If
u=2
, then
uf(u)=4
, and
u+v=2+m^{2}
cannot be a square. So
f(u+v)\geq 3
. Then
(
155
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{3}\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25\cdot 2\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{36}{23}<2,

so
w^{\prime}=1
. But then (
156
) gives

\displaystyle f(u+v)<16\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25\cdot 2\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{108}{23}<5.

So
f(u+v)=3
. Then (
153
) gives

3(2vy^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4(2z^{\prime 2}+4y^{\prime\prime 2})
, so

(3v-8)y^{\prime\prime 2}=4z^{\prime 2}+24.

So as
v
is odd,
y^{\prime\prime}
must be even, say
y^{\prime\prime}=2y^{\prime\prime\prime}
, so
(3v-8)y^{\prime\prime\prime 2}=z^{\prime 2}+6
. But it is easy to see that as
v
is an odd square, this equation has no solution modulo 8.

We conclude from the above that
v=9
. In this case, for
u\geq 2
, (
156
) gives

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}<16\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{9\cdot 2}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{9\cdot 2\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{44}{7}<7.

Notice that
f(u+v)\not=3
since
\gcd(u+v,v)=1
. So we have three possibilities:

(a)

f(u+v)=1
and
w^{\prime}=1
,

(b)

f(u+v)=1
and
w^{\prime}=2
,

(c)

f(u+v)=5
and
w^{\prime}=1
.

In these cases, (
153
) gives respectively

(157)

\displaystyle y^{\prime\prime 2}

\displaystyle=4z^{\prime 2}+8,

(158)

\displaystyle 7y^{\prime\prime 2}

\displaystyle=z^{\prime 2}+8,

(159)

\displaystyle 37y^{\prime\prime 2}

\displaystyle=4z^{\prime 2}+40.

However, one finds that Equation (
157
) has no solution modulo 16,
Equation (
158
) has no solution modulo 32, and
Equation (
159
) has no solution modulo 25.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 31
.

If
y^{\prime\prime}=1
, then
v=1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
29
,
f(v)=1
, so
v
is an odd square, say
v=m^{2}
.
Suppose
y^{\prime\prime}=1
and that
v\geq 9
.
First note that if
f(u)>2
, then
f(u)\geq 6
, and so
uf(u)\geq 36
. Then by (
152
),

4v>uf(u)z^{\prime 2}\geq uf(u)\geq 36
, so
v>9
. Thus
v\geq 25
and then
(
156
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{36}+\frac{1}{25\cdot 6}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25\cdot 6-16}\right)=\frac{124}{201}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(u)=2
.
Then (
153
) gives

(160)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(m^{2}-8)=4(z^{\prime 2}+2).

In particular, as
m
and
f(u+v)
are odd,
w^{\prime}
must be even.
For all
u\geq 2
, (
156
) gives

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}<16\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{9\cdot 2}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{9\cdot 2-16}\right)=44.

So, as
w^{\prime}
is even and
f(u+v)
is odd and square-free, we have three possibilities:

(a)

w^{\prime}=6
and
f(u+v)=1
; here (
160
) gives
9m^{2}=z^{\prime 2}+74
.

(b)

w^{\prime}=4
and
f(u+v)=1
; here (
160
) gives
4(m^{2}-8)=z^{\prime 2}+2
.

(c)

w^{\prime}=2
and
f(u+v)=1,3,5,7
; here (
160
) gives
f(u+v)(m^{2}-8)=z^{\prime 2}+2
.

However, in the first two cases, the equation has no solution modulo 4.
In the third case we find that for
f(u+v)=1
and
5
, the equation
f(u+v)(m^{2}-8)=z^{\prime 2}+2
also has no solution modulo 4,
while for
f(u+v)=7
, the equation
f(u+v)(m^{2}-8)=z^{\prime 2}+2
has no solution modulo 8.
So it remains to treat the case where
f(u+v)=3
and
w^{\prime}=2
.
So it remains to treat the case where
f(u+v)=3
and
w^{\prime}=2
.
Here the equation is
3m^{2}=z^{\prime 2}+26
, which actually does have integer solutions. However, notice that for
f(u+v)=3
, we have
v\not=9
, since
\gcd(u+v,v)=1
, so
v\geq 25
. Hence for
u\geq 2
,
(
155
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{3}\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{25\cdot 2}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25\cdot 2-16}\right)=\frac{36}{17}<3.

But this contradicts the assumption that
w^{\prime}=2
.
∎

By the two preceding lemmas,
v=1
.

Lemma 32
.

y^{\prime\prime}>1
.

Proof.

If
y^{\prime\prime}=1
, then by (
152
), we have
uf(u)z^{\prime 2}<4
. But this is impossible as
uf(u)\geq 4
.
∎

Lemma 33
.

If
f(u+1)>1
, then
f(u)=2
.

Proof.

By the previous lemma, we have
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
. Suppose
f(u+1)>1
.
Note that if
f(u)\geq 10
, then
f(u+1)\geq 3
and
(
155
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{3}\left(\frac{1}{10^{2}}+\frac{1}{10}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{10\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{44}{45}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Furthermore, if
f(u)=6
and
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 3
, then as
\gcd(u,u+1)=1
, we have
f(u+1)\not=3
, so
f(u+1)\geq 5
, and hence
(
155
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{5}\left(\frac{1}{6^{2}}+\frac{1}{6}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{6\cdot 9-16}\right)=\frac{84}{95}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Finally, suppose that
f(u)=6
and
y^{\prime\prime}=2
. Then
f(u+1)\geq 5
and (
155
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{5}\left(\frac{1}{6^{2}}+\frac{1}{6}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{6\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{28}{15}<2.

So
w^{\prime}=1
. Then applying (
155
) again gives

\displaystyle f(u+1)

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{6^{2}}+\frac{1}{6}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{6\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{28}{3}<10,

So as
f(u+1)
is odd and square-free and

, we have
f(u+1)=5
or
7
. Hence from (

) we have

, which is impossible for
f(u+1)=5
and
7
.
∎

Lemma 34
.

If
f(u+1)=1
, then
f(u)=2
.

Proof.

Suppose
f(u+1)=1
, so
u+1=r^{2}
for some odd
r
. So
u\equiv 0\pmod{8}
.
Suppose that
f(u)>2
. Then
f(u)\geq 6
and as
u
is divisible by 8,

u\geq 24
. So, as
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
by Lemma
32
,
(
156
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{24\cdot 6}+\frac{1}{6}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{6\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{25}{3}<9.

So
w^{\prime}=1
or
2
. First suppose
w^{\prime}=1
. Then (
153
) gives

(f(u)-16)y^{\prime\prime 2}=4f(u)z^{\prime 2}+16
.
In particular,
f(u)>16
. Thus, as
f(u)
is even and square-free,
f(u)\geq 22
. So, as
u
is divisible by 8,

. But then
(
156
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{88\cdot 22}+\frac{1}{22}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{22\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{89}{99}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
w^{\prime}=2
. Then (
153
) gives

(161)

(f(u)-4)y^{\prime\prime 2}=f(u)z^{\prime 2}+16.

By (

), we have
4y^{\prime\prime 2}>uf(u)z^{\prime 2}\geq 24f(u)z^{\prime 2}
. So (

) gives

and hence

. But

and

, so in fact, as

is even and square-free, the only possibility is

Lemma 35
.

u=2
.

In the first case, with

In the third case, the equation is
y^{\prime\prime 2}=4z^{\prime 2}+72
has no solution modulo 16.

∎

From the preceding lemmas, we have
v=1
and
u=2
. By (

. So, as

. But then

. Note that in this case

\Sigma=\frac{1}{2}f(u+v)f(u)vf(v)w^{\prime 2}=3w^{\prime 2}=12,

and
\Sigma^{\prime}=\frac{u}{v}\Sigma=24
. This is our desired Case 4 solution.

Case 5. Assume
u,v
are both odd and the 2-adic order of
u+v
is even.

We will show that there are no solutions in this case.

As
x
is an integer,
from (
109
) we can write
\Sigma=2f(u+v)f(u)w^{2}
, for some
w
.
Note
v
divides
\Sigma
, so
v
divides
w^{2}
, and hence
f(v)s(v)
divides
w
. Thus, setting

we may write
\Sigma=2f(u+v)f(u)f(v)vw^{\prime 2}
. Then
(

) gives

(164)

f(u+v)f(u)f(v)vw^{\prime 2}(vf(u)y^{\prime 2}-16)=4(z^{2}+vf(u)y^{\prime 2}).

Thus

divides

, so

divides

, say

. So (

and hence

(165)

s(v)y^{\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime},

and (

) gives

Hence
f(v)
divides
y^{\prime}
. Let
y^{\prime}=f(v)y^{\prime\prime}
.
Then (

) gives

f(v)s(v)y^{\prime\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime}\quad\text{and so}\quad vf(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}>uf(u)z^{\prime 2},

and (

) gives

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4(f(u)z^{\prime 2}+f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}).

Now (
167
) and (
168
) give

uf(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)<4(v+u)f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}.

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{4}{f(v+u)}\left(\frac{1}{uf(u)f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)f(u)}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16}\right)

and consequently

Remark 27
.

Note that using the hypothesis that the 2-adic order of
u+v
is even, one has

f(u)+f(v)\equiv u+v\equiv 0\ \pmod{4}.

In particular,
f(u)
and
f(v)
are not both 1.

.

Lemma 37
.

f(v)=1

Proof.

. By Lemma

. Then by Remark

, and in particular,

. Then (

or

.

. So

. In particular,

, and modulo 3 we have

∎

.

168
) gives
f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(3y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4(3z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2})
, and thus modulo 3 we obtain

y^{\prime\prime 2}\equiv-1
, which is impossible. So there there are no solutions in Case 5.

Case 6. Assume
u,v
are both odd and the 2-adic order of
u+v
is odd.

We will show that in this case, one of the following holds:

(a)

(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})=(9,9)
or
(16,16)
,

(b)

(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})=(m^{2},1)
, for some integer
m
satisfying the equations
m^{2}+1=2n^{2}
and

(m^{2}-8)Y^{2}=1+8Z^{2}
for some integers
n,Y,Z
,

(c)

(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})=(5m^{2},5)
, for some integer
m
satisfying the equations
m^{2}+1=10n^{2}
and

(5m^{2}-8)Y^{2}=5+8Z^{2}
for some integers
n,Y,Z
.

As
x
is an integer,
from (
109
) we can write
2\Sigma=f(u+v)f(u)w^{2}
.
Note
v
divides
\Sigma
, so
v
divides
w^{2}
, and hence
f(v)s(v)
divides
w
. Thus, setting
w=f(v)s(v)w^{\prime}
we may write
2\Sigma=f(u+v)f(u)f(v)vw^{\prime 2}
. Then
(
108
) gives

(171)

f(u+v)f(u)f(v)vw^{\prime 2}(vf(u)y^{\prime 2}-16)=16(z^{2}+vf(u)y^{\prime 2}).

Thus
vf(u)
divides
16z^{2}
, so
f(v)s(v)f(u)
divides
z
, say
z=f(v)s(v)f(u)z^{\prime}
. So (
107
) gives
vy^{\prime}>s(u)f(v)s(v)f(u)z^{\prime}
and hence

(172)

s(v)y^{\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime},

and (
171
) gives

(173)

f(u+v)f(v)w^{\prime 2}(vf(u)y^{\prime 2}-16)=16(f(v)f(u)z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime 2}).

Hence
f(v)
divides
y^{\prime}
. Let
y^{\prime}=f(v)y^{\prime\prime}
.
Then (
172
) gives

(174)

f(v)s(v)y^{\prime\prime}>f(u)s(u)z^{\prime}\quad\text{and so}\quad vf(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}>uf(u)z^{\prime 2},

and (
173
) gives

(175)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=16(f(u)z^{\prime 2}+f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}).

Note that from the left-hand side of (
175
), we have

(176)

vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}>16.

Furthermore, (
174
) and (
175
) give

uf(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)<16(v+u)f(v)y^{\prime\prime 2}.

Hence

(177)

w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16}{f(u+v)}\left(\frac{1}{uf(u)f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)f(u)}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16}\right)

and consequently, as
f(u+v)\geq 2
,

(178)

w^{\prime 2}<8\left(\frac{1}{uf(u)f(v)}+\frac{1}{vf(v)f(u)}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{vf^{2}(v)f(u)y^{\prime\prime 2}-16}\right).

Remark 28
.

As the 2-adic order of
u+v
is odd, one has

f(u)+f(v)\equiv u+v\equiv 2\ \pmod{4}.

Lemma 40
.

The following conditions hold.

(a)

f(u)\leq 23
.

(b)

f(v)\leq 7
.

(c)

If
y^{\prime\prime}>1
, then

f(u)\leq 11
.

Proof.

(a). If
f(u)>23
, then as
f(u)
is square-free,
f(u)\geq 29
, so for all
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 1
, (
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{29^{2}}+\frac{1}{29}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{29-16}\right)=\frac{240}{377}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

(b). If
f(v)>7
, then as
f(v)
is square-free,
f(v)\geq 11
, and (
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{121}+\frac{1}{11}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{11^{3}-16}\right)=\frac{1056}{1315}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

(c). If
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2

and
f(u)\geq 13
, then (
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{13^{2}}+\frac{1}{13}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{13\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{112}{117}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

∎

Lemma 41
.

f(v)=1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
40
(b),
f(v)\leq 7
. First suppose
f(v)=7
. Then by Remark
28
,
f(u)\equiv 3\pmod{4}
, and in particular,
f(u)\geq 3
and so
u\geq 3
. Then (
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{49\cdot 3}+\frac{1}{7\cdot 9}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{7^{3}\cdot 3-16}\right)=\frac{560}{3039}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(v)\leq 5
.

Now suppose that
f(v)=5
. Then by Remark
28
,
f(u)\equiv 1\pmod{4}
. Suppose for the moment that
f(u)>1
. Then as
u,v
are relatively prime and
v
is divisible by
5
, we have
f(u)\geq 13
. So
(
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{13^{2}\cdot 5}+\frac{1}{5^{2}\cdot 13}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{5^{3}\cdot 13-16}\right)=\frac{720}{20917}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(u)=1
. Hence
u
is an odd square. Suppose for the moment that
u>1
. Then
u\geq 9
and
(
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{9\cdot 5}+\frac{1}{5^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{5^{3}-16}\right)=\frac{560}{981}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
u=1
. Notice that as
f(v)=5
we have
v=5m^{2}
for some odd
m
and so
u+v=1+5m^{2}
. In particular,
f(u+v)\not=2
as otherwise

1+5m^{2}=2r^{2}
for some
r
. But this equation has no solution modulo 5. So, as the 2-adic order of
u+v
is odd, we have
f(u+v)\geq 6
.
Then (
177
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{6}\left(\frac{1}{5}+\frac{1}{5^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{5^{3}-16}\right)=\frac{80}{109}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(v)\not=5
.

Now suppose that
f(v)=3
. By Remark
28
,
f(u)\equiv 3\pmod{4}
. So as
u,v
are relatively prime, and as
v
is divisible by
3
, we have
f(u)\not=3
, so
f(u)\geq 7
.
Then (
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{7^{2}\cdot 3}+\frac{1}{3^{2}\cdot 7}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{3^{3}\cdot 7-16}\right)=\frac{240}{1211}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(v)=1
.
∎

Lemma 42
.

If
v=1
, then
f(u)=1
.

Proof.

Suppose
v=1
.
From (
174
) we have
y^{\prime\prime}>f(u)z^{\prime}\geq 1
.
Thus from Lemma
40
(c) we have
f(u)\leq 11
.
Moreover, as
v=1
, we have
f(u)\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, by Remark
28
.
Thus, as
f(u)
is square-free,
f(u)\leq 5
.
Suppose
f(u)=5
. Then from (
174
) we have
y^{\prime\prime}>5z^{\prime}\geq 5
. So
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 6
.
Then (
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{5^{2}}+\frac{1}{5}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{5\cdot 6^{2}-16}\right)=\frac{432}{205}<3.

So
w^{\prime}=1
. Then (
175
) gives
f(u+v)(5y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=16(5z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2})
, so

(5f(u+v)-16)y^{\prime\prime 2}=80z^{\prime 2}+16f(u+v)>0.

So
f(u+v)>16/5
. Thus, as
u+v
is even and square-free,
f(u+v)\geq 6
. Hence (
177
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{6}\left(\frac{1}{5^{2}}+\frac{1}{5}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{5\cdot 6^{2}-16}\right)=\frac{144}{205}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(u)<5
and since
f(u)\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, we have
f(u)=1
.
∎

Lemma 43
.

If
v=1
, then
u=1
and
w^{\prime}=3
or
4
.

Proof.

Suppose
v=1
. By the previous lemma,
f(u)=1
. Then (
175
) gives
f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=16(z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2})
, and so

(179)

(f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}-16)y^{\prime\prime 2}=16(z^{\prime 2}+f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}).

From (
176
), we have
y^{\prime\prime}>4
. Thus
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 5
.
So (
177
) gives

(180)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}<16\left(\frac{1}{u}+1\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25-16}\right)=\frac{16\cdot 25}{9}\left(\frac{1}{u}+1\right).

In particular, as
w^{\prime}\geq 1
and
u\geq 1
, this gives
f(u+v)<\frac{16\cdot 25\cdot 2}{9}=\frac{800}{9}
, so
f(u+v)\leq 88
. Moreover,
f(u+v)
is even and square-free, and furthermore,
as
u
is an odd square, say
u=n^{2}
, and
n^{2}+1=f(u+v)m^{2}
, where
m=s(u+v)
, we have that
-1
is a quadratic residue modulo
f(u+v)
. Hence
f(u+v)
cannot be divisible by a prime congruent to 3 modulo 4. It follows that the only possible values of
f(u+v)
are:

2,10,26,34,58,74,82.

Notice also that by (
179
), we have
f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}>16
, so
w^{\prime}\geq 2
for
f(u+v)=10
and
w^{\prime}\geq 3
for
f(u+v)=2
.

Let us assume for the moment that
u>1
. So, as
f(u)=1
, we have
u\geq 9
. Then from (
174
), we have
y^{\prime\prime 2}>uz^{\prime 2}\geq 9z^{\prime 2}
.
Moreover, (
180
) gives
f(u+v)<\frac{16\cdot 25}{9}(\frac{1}{9}+1)=\frac{4000}{81}<50
, so
f(u+v)\leq 34
. For the resulting four cases of
f(u+v)
we have:

(a)

If
f(u+v)=34
, then (
180
) gives
w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16\cdot 25}{34\cdot 9}(\frac{1}{9}+1)=\frac{2000}{1377}<2
, so
w^{\prime}=1
. Then (
179
) gives
9y^{\prime\prime 2}=8(z^{\prime 2}+34)
, which is impossible modulo 3.

(b)

If
f(u+v)=26
, then (
180
) gives
w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16\cdot 25}{26\cdot 9}(\frac{1}{9}+1)=\frac{2000}{1053}<2
, so
w^{\prime}=1
. Then (
179
) gives
5y^{\prime\prime 2}=8(z^{\prime 2}+26)
. So as
y^{\prime\prime 2}>9z^{\prime 2}
, we have

45z^{\prime\prime 2}<8(z^{\prime 2}+26)
; i.e.,
z^{\prime\prime 2}<\frac{8\cdot 26}{37}
, so
z^{\prime\prime}\leq 2
. But
5y^{\prime\prime 2}=8(z^{\prime 2}+26)
has no integer solution for
y^{\prime\prime}
when
z^{\prime}=1

or
z^{\prime}=2
.

(c)

If
f(u+v)=10
, then (
180
) gives
w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16\cdot 25}{10\cdot 9}(\frac{1}{9}+1)=\frac{400}{81}<5
, so
w^{\prime}\leq 2
.
But
w^{\prime}\geq 2
for
f(u+v)=10
, as we observed above, so
w^{\prime}=2
. Then (
179
) gives
3y^{\prime\prime 2}=2(z^{\prime 2}+40)
, which is impossible modulo 3.

(d)

If
f(u+v)=2
, then (
180
) gives
w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16\cdot 25}{2\cdot 9}(\frac{1}{9}+1)=\frac{2000}{81}<25
, so
w^{\prime}\leq 4
.
But
w^{\prime}\geq 3
for
f(u+v)=2
, as we observed above, so
w^{\prime}=3
or
4
.

(i)

If
w^{\prime}=3
, then (
179
) gives

y^{\prime\prime 2}=8(z^{\prime 2}+18)
. But then
y^{\prime\prime 2}>9z^{\prime 2}
gives
z^{\prime 2}<8\cdot 18
, so
z^{\prime}\leq 11
. But for none of these values does
y^{\prime\prime 2}=8(z^{\prime 2}+18)
have an integer solution for
y^{\prime\prime}
. So
this case is impossible.

(ii)

If
w^{\prime}=4
, then (
179
) gives

y^{\prime\prime 2}=z^{\prime 2}+32
. But then
y^{\prime\prime 2}>9z^{\prime 2}
gives
z^{\prime 2}<4
, so
z^{\prime}=1
. However then
y^{\prime\prime 2}=z^{\prime 2}+32
has no integer solution for
y^{\prime\prime}
. So this case is also impossible.

We conclude from the above that
u=1
. So
f(u+v)=2
and
(
180
) gives
w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16\cdot 25\cdot 2}{2\cdot 9}=\frac{400}{9}<45
, so
w^{\prime}\leq 6
.
But
w^{\prime}\geq 3
for
f(u+v)=2
, as we observed above, so
w^{\prime}=3,4,5
or
6
. We will now eliminate the possibilities that
w^{\prime}=5
or
6
.

(a)

If
w^{\prime}=5
, then (
179
) gives
17y^{\prime\prime 2}=8(z^{\prime 2}+50)
. From (
174
),
y^{\prime\prime 2}>uz^{\prime 2}\geq z^{\prime 2}
. So

9z^{\prime\prime 2}<8\cdot 50
and hence
z^{\prime\prime}\leq 6
. But for none of these values does
17y^{\prime\prime 2}=8(z^{\prime 2}+50)
have an integer solution for
y^{\prime\prime}
. So
this case is impossible.

(b)

If
w^{\prime}=6
, then (
179
) gives
7y^{\prime\prime 2}=2(z^{\prime 2}+72)
, which is impossible mod 7.

∎

Lemma 44
.

If
v>1
, then
u=1
,
w^{\prime}=1
and either
f(u+v)=2
or
f(u+v)=10
.

Proof.

By the previous lemma,
f(v)=1
, so
v
is an odd square,
v=m^{2}
say. Suppose
m\geq 3
.
Note that
f(u)\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, by Remark
28
. If
f(u)\geq 5
, then (
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{5^{2}}+\frac{1}{5\cdot 9}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{9\cdot 5-16}\right)=\frac{112}{145}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
f(u)<5
and since
f(u)\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, we have
f(u)=1
.

First suppose that
v=9
.
Substituting
v=9,f(u)=1
in (
175
) gives
f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(9y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=16(z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2})
, and so

(181)

(9f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}-16)y^{\prime\prime 2}=16(z^{\prime 2}+f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}).

From (
176
), we have
9y^{\prime\prime 2}>16
. Thus
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
.
So (
177
) gives

(182)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}<16\left(\frac{1}{u}+\frac{1}{9}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{9\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{16\cdot 9}{5}\left(\frac{1}{u}+\frac{1}{9}\right).

Let us assume for the moment that
u>1
. So, as
f(u)=1
and
\gcd(u,v)=1
, we have
u\geq 25
. Then
w^{\prime}\geq 1
and (
182
) give
f(u+v)<\frac{16\cdot 9}{5}(\frac{1}{25}+\frac{1}{9})=\frac{544}{125}<5
so, as
f(u+v)
is even and square-free,
f(u+v)=2
. Then we have

m^{2}+9=2n^{2}
for some
n
. But considering this equation modulo 3, it follows that
n,m
are both divisible by 3, contradicting the hypothesis that
\gcd(u,v)=1
. So
u=1
.
Thus,
f(u+v)=u+v=10
. Furthermore, (
182
) gives
w^{\prime 2}<\frac{16\cdot 9}{10\cdot 5}(1+\frac{1}{9})=\frac{16}{5}<4
, so
w^{\prime}=1
, as required.

Now suppose that
v>9
, so
v\geq 25
. As
f(u)=1
, so
u,v
are both odd squares.
First suppose that if
u\geq 49
. Then since
v\geq 25
,
(
177
) gives

\displaystyle f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<16\left(\frac{1}{49}+\frac{1}{25}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25-16}\right)=\frac{1184}{441}<3.

So necessarily
f(u+v)=2
and
w^{\prime}=1
. Then (
175
) gives
vy^{\prime\prime 2}-16=8(z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2})
, so as
v
is odd,
y^{\prime\prime}
is divisible by 4.
But then
(
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{49}+\frac{1}{25}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25\cdot 4^{2}-16}\right)=\frac{74}{147}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Hence
u=1,9
or
25
. If
u=25
, then since
u,v
are relatively prime, we have
v\geq 49
and thus
(
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{25}+\frac{1}{49}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{49-16}\right)=\frac{592}{825}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Hence
u=1
or
9
.

Suppose
u=9
. Then
u+v=9+m^{2}
, and if
f(u+v)=2
, then
u+v=2r^{2}
, for some
r
, and thus
9+m^{2}=2r^{2}
. Modulo 3 this would give
m\equiv 0\pmod{3}
, contradicting the assumption that
u,v
are relatively prime. Hence
f(u+v)>2
. In this case, using again the fact that
u,v
are relatively prime, we would have
f(u+v)\geq 10
. Thus,
if
v\geq 49
, then (
177
) would give

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{10}\left(\frac{1}{9}+\frac{1}{49}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{49-16}\right)=\frac{464}{1485}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

Hence
v=25
. But in this case,
f(u+v)=34
and (
177
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{34}\left(\frac{1}{9}+\frac{1}{25}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25-16}\right)=\frac{16}{81}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
u\not=9
.

Finally, suppose
u=1
.
Thus (
175
) gives

(183)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vy^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=16(z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2}).

First suppose that
v=25
. Then
f(1+v)=26
. Note that if
y^{\prime\prime}>1
, then (
177
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{26}\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{25}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{16}{21}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So
y^{\prime\prime}=1
. Then (
177
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{26}\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{25}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{25-16}\right)=\frac{16}{9}<2,

so
w^{\prime}=1
. Substituting in (
183
) gives
8z^{\prime\prime 2}=109
, which has no integer solution. Hence
v>25
and thus
v\geq 49
.

For
v\geq 49
, (
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{49}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{49-16}\right)=\frac{400}{33}<13,

so
w^{\prime}\leq 3
. Suppose for the moment that
y^{\prime\prime}
is odd. Then working modulo 4, as
v
is odd and
f(u+v)\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, we conclude from (
183
)
that
w^{\prime}
is even. So
w^{\prime}\leq 3
gives
w^{\prime}=2
. Then (
183
) gives

f(u+v)(vy^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=4(z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2})
,
and working modulo 4 again gives a contradiction. Thus
y^{\prime\prime}
is even, say
y^{\prime\prime}=2y^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and (
183
) gives

(184)

f(u+v)w^{\prime 2}(vy^{\prime\prime\prime 2}-4)=4(z^{\prime 2}+4y^{\prime\prime\prime 2}).

As
y^{\prime\prime}=2y^{\prime\prime\prime}\geq 2
, (
178
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<8\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{49}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{49\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{80}{9}<9,

so
w^{\prime}\leq 2
.

Note that if
f(u+v)\geq 18
, then for
y^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
, (
177
) would give

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{18}\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{49}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{49\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{80}{81}<1.\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\!\Leftarrow

So as
f(u+v)
is even and square-free,
f(u+v)=2,6,10
or
14
. But
u+v=1+m^{2}
and
-1
is not quadratic residue modulo 6 or 14. So

f(u+v)=2
or
10
. If
f(u+v)=10
, then (
177
) gives

\displaystyle w^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle<\frac{16}{10}\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{49}\right)\left(1+\frac{16}{49\cdot 4-16}\right)=\frac{16}{9}<2,

so
w^{\prime}=1
. We will show that one also has
w^{\prime}=1
when
f(u+v)=2
. Indeed, suppose
f(u+v)=2
and
w^{\prime}=2
. Then (
184
) gives
2(vy^{\prime\prime\prime 2}-4)=z^{\prime 2}+4y^{\prime\prime\prime 2}
. As
f(u+v)=2
, we have
v=m^{2}=2n^{2}-1
for some
n
. So we have

2(2n^{2}-3)y^{\prime\prime\prime 2}-8=z^{\prime 2}.

However, this equation has no solution for
n,y^{\prime\prime\prime},z^{\prime}
modulo 64. So
w^{\prime}=1
.
∎

If
v=1
, then from Lemma
43
,
u=1
and
w^{\prime}=3
or
4
.
Then (
175
) gives
w^{\prime 2}(y^{\prime\prime 2}-16)=8(z^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime\prime 2})
.

(a)

If
w^{\prime}=3
, then we have
y^{\prime\prime 2}=8(z^{\prime 2}+18)
. This equation has infinitely many solutions. Here
\Sigma=\frac{1}{2}f(u+v)f(u)vf(v)w^{\prime 2}=9
, and
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma
. So this is one of our desired solutions.

(b)

If
w^{\prime}=4
, then we have
y^{\prime\prime 2}=z^{\prime 2}+32
. This has two solutions (
y^{\prime\prime}=6,z^{\prime}=2
and
y^{\prime\prime}=9,z^{\prime}=7
). Here
\Sigma=\frac{1}{2}f(u+v)f(u)vf(v)w^{\prime 2}=16
, and
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma
. So this is another one of our desired solutions.

If
v>1
, then from Lemma
41
,
f(v)=1
so
v=m^{2}
for some odd
m
, and from Lemma
44
,
u=1
,
w^{\prime}=1
and either
f(u+v)=2
or
f(u+v)=10
.
Then (
175
) gives

(f(u+v)m^{2}-16)y^{\prime\prime 2}=16f(u+v)+16z^{\prime 2}
.
Then as
m
is odd and
f(u+v)=2
or
10
, we have that
y^{\prime\prime}
is divisible by 4, say
y^{\prime\prime}=4Y
. So

(f(u+v)m^{2}-16)Y^{2}=f(u+v)+z^{\prime 2}
.
Working mod 8 we see that
Y
is necessarily odd and
z^{\prime 2}\equiv 0
, so
z^{\prime}
is divisible by 4, say
z^{\prime}=4Z
. So we have

(185)

\big(\frac{f(u+v)}{2}m^{2}-8\big)Y^{2}=\frac{f(u+v)}{2}+8Z^{2}.

Furthermore,
\Sigma=\frac{1}{2}f(u+v)f(u)vf(v)w^{\prime 2}=\frac{f(u+v)}{2}m^{2}
, and
\Sigma^{\prime}=\frac{u}{v}\Sigma=\frac{f(u+v)}{2}
.

Thus when
f(u+v)=2
, we have
(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})=(m^{2},1)
. Furthermore, there exists
n
such that

(186)

m^{2}+1=2n^{2}\ \text{and}\ (m^{2}-8)Y^{2}=1+8Z^{2},

where the latter equation comes from (
185
). Similarly,

f(u+v)=10
, there exists
n
such that

(187)

m^{2}+1=10n^{2}\ \text{and}\ (5m^{2}-8)Y^{2}=5+8Z^{2}.

So these are the two desired families of solutions. This completes the proof of Theorem
4
.

Remark 29
.

Consider the integers
m
for which there exists
n
with
m^{2}+1=10n^{2}
, as in (
187
). It is easy to see that
m
is necessarily divisible by 3. The numbers
m/3
are well known; see entry
A097314 of
[
35
]
. The first eight values of
m
are:
3,117,4443,168717,6406803,243289797,3079535161,116941239519
.

Remark 30
.

Suppose that in the case
u=1,v=m^{2}
at the end of the above proof, we have a solution
m,n,Y,Z
to (
186
) or (
187
). From (
109
), as
y=y^{\prime}=y^{\prime\prime}=4Y
, and
u+v=1+m^{2}=f(u+v)n^{2}
and
\Sigma=\frac{f(u+v)}{2}m^{2}
,

x=\sqrt{\frac{y^{\prime 2}f(u)(u+v)\Sigma}{8}}=f(u+v)mnY.

Moreover,
z=f(v)s(v)f(u)z^{\prime}=mz^{\prime}=4mZ
and from Definition
6
,

x=a+b,y=a-c,z=c-b
.

For
f(u+v)=2
, we have
x=2mnY
and solving gives, using
d=a+b-c
,

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=(mn+2)Y+2mZ,\qquad b=(mn-2)Y-2mZ,

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=(mn-2)Y+2mZ,\qquad d=(mn+2)Y-2mZ.

Notice that as
m<\sqrt{2}n
, we have
\Sigma=m^{2}<4mn=8\frac{a+b}{a-c}
.
Hence by Remark
18
, all such extangential LEQ (if any exist) are necessarily convex.

Similarly, for
f(u+v)=10
, we have
x=10mnY
and solving gives

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=(5mn+2)Y+2mZ,\qquad b=(5mn-2)Y-2mZ,

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=(5mn-2)Y+2mZ,\qquad d=(5mn+2)Y-2mZ.

Notice that as
m<\sqrt{10}n
, we have
\Sigma=5m^{2}<20mn=8\frac{a+b}{a-c}
.
Hence by Remark
18
, all such extangential LEQ are necessarily convex.

Remark 31
.

Note that we now have all the ingredients for the proof of Corollary
3
from the introduction.
The proof for the LEQs of Theorem
3
parts (b) and (c) are given in the previous remark.
The proof for the LEQs of Theorem
3
part (a) were given in Section
9
; see Remark
19
and the analysis of LEQs with
(\Sigma,T)=(18,50)
.

Remark 32
.

We mention that in the case
m=3
of (
187
), the equation
(5m^{2}-8)Y^{2}=5+8Z^{2}
gives

37Y^{2}=5+8Z^{2}
, which is equivalent to Equation (
99
) in Section
9
;
the connection is given by setting
W=111Y+52Z
.

Example 5
.

We now exhibit an extangential LEQ corresponding to the case
m=117
of (
187
). This is the case
n=37
in Theorem
3
(b). According to
[
5
]
, the smallest solution to
(5\cdot 117^{2}-8)Y^{2}=5+8Z^{2}
is

Y=34884218483995340806373,\quad Z=3226483779786979759026161.

The formulas from Remark
30
give

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=1510135881993200406047678005,\quad b=1936178957897460209165

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=1509996345119264424684452513,\quad d=141473052893878823434657.

Let

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=(640848245491383541211578005,1367415046112187810865469000),

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=(640849067137238673279485480,1367416799305572965277883040),

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=(60036158873125939312368,128102631990427959679265).

It is easy to verify that the points
O,A,B,C
form the vertices of an extangential LEQ with side lengths
a,b,c,d
as given above (see below).
This LEQ has
(\Sigma,T)=(5\cdot 117^{2},5+5\cdot 117^{2})
. Note the perimeter is
a+b+c+d\cong 3.0\cdot 10^{27}
.

Let us briefly explain how the above vertices were determined. First factorize
a
and note that each prime factor is congruent to 1 mod 4. Then for each factor
\alpha
of
a
, consider all ways of writing
\alpha
as a sum of two squares:
\alpha=\alpha_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}^{2}
, where
\alpha_{1}>\alpha_{2}>0
, and using the Pythagorean formula, consider the points
A_{\alpha}=\frac{a}{\alpha}(\alpha_{1}^{2}-\alpha_{2}^{2},2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})
and
A_{\alpha}^{*}=\frac{a}{\alpha}(2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}^{2}-\alpha_{2}^{2})
. Let
P_{a}
denote the union over
\alpha
of all the sets
\{A_{\alpha},A_{\alpha}^{*}\}
. Similarly, construct
P_{b},P_{c}
and
P_{d}
. Then search for
members
S_{a},S_{b},S_{c},S_{d}
in
P_{a},P_{b},P_{c},P_{d}
respectively such that
S_{a}+S_{b}=S_{c}+S_{d}
, and set
A=S_{a},B=S_{a}+S_{b},C=S_{c}
.
By construction, the resulting quadrilateral
OABC
has side lengths
a,b,c,d
. The extangential condition,
a+b=c+d
, is verified directly from the above values of
a,b,c,d
.
Finally, check that

\det[S_{a},S_{b}]>0\ \text{and}\ \det[S_{c},S_{d}]>0,

which shows that
OABC
is positively oriented and has no self-intersection, and check that the equability condition is satisfied, i.e.,

a+b+c+d=\frac{1}{2}\big(\det[S_{a},S_{b}]+\det[S_{c},S_{d}]\big).

12.
Comments on the Open Problem

In this section we make some comments on the Open Problem stated in the Introduction.
Suppose we have integers
m,n,Y,Z
such that the following two equations hold:

(188)

\displaystyle m^{2}

\displaystyle=2n^{2}-1,

(189)

\displaystyle(m^{2}-8)Y^{2}

\displaystyle=1+8Z^{2}.

For convenience, set
M=m^{2}-8
. We first make some elementary observations:

1.

From (
188
),
m
is odd. Then working modulo 4, as
m^{2}=2n^{2}-1
and
m
odd,
n
is also odd, and hence from (
189
),
Y
and
M
are also odd.

2.

Working modulo 3,
2n^{2}-1\equiv\pm 1
. So from (
188
),
m
is not divisible by 3.
Thus
m^{2}\equiv 1
, so from (
188
) again,
n
is not divisible by 3. Hence
M\equiv 2
. Thus, from (
189
),
Y
is necessarily divisible by 3, and
Z
is not divisible by 3.

3.

Working modulo 7, the quadratic residues are
0,1,2
and
4
. So
2n^{2}-1
is
0,\pm 1
or
3
. So as
m^{2}=2n^{2}-1
, we conclude
m^{2}
is
0
or
1
. Then
M\equiv-1
or
0
. But if
M\equiv 0
then (
189
) gives
Z^{2}\equiv-1
, which is impossible. So
m
is divisible by 7.

4.

The prime divisors of
m^{2}-8
are all congruent to 1 mod 8.
Indeed, suppose
p
is a prime divisor of
m^{2}-8
. Then
8\equiv m^{2}\pmod{p}
. But it is well known that
8
is a quadratic residue mod an odd prime
p
if and only if
p
is congruent to 1 or 7 mod 8. So
p
is congruent to 1 or 7 mod 8. But by (
189
),
p
is also a prime divisor of
1+8Z^{2}
, so
-2\equiv(4Z)^{2}\pmod{p}
. But it is well known that
-2
is a quadratic residue mod an odd prime
p
if and only if
p
is congruent to 1 or 3 mod 8. Hence
p
is congruent to 1 mod 8.

Let
m=2r+1
. Then
m^{2}=2n^{2}-1
gives
4r^{2}+4r+2=2n^{2}
, so
r^{2}+(r+1)^{2}=n^{2}
. So the solutions
(m,n)
to (
188
) correspond to Pythagorean triangles
(r,r+1,n)
whose base and height differ by 1. These triangles are well known; see entry A001652 of
[
35
]
. In particular, it is well known that the solutions
r_{0},r_{1},r_{2},\dots
satisfy
r_{i}=6r_{i-1}-r_{i-2}+2
with
r_{0}=0,r_{1}=3
. Let us denote the correspond values of
m
by
m_{i}=2r_{i}+1
. So
m_{i}=6m_{i-1}-m_{i-2}
with
m_{0}=1,m_{1}=7
. As we saw in observation 3 above, we are only interested in values of
m
that are divisible by 7. Note that modulo 7,

m_{i}\equiv-m_{i-1}-m_{i-2}
, so
m_{i+1}\equiv-m_{i}-m_{i-1}\equiv m_{i-2}
. So, as
m_{0}\equiv 1,m_{1}\equiv 0,m_{2}\equiv-1
, we are only interested in the values
m_{1+3i}
. Set
\mu_{i}:=\frac{1}{7}m_{1+3i}
.
The sequence
\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots
is also well known; see entry A097732 of
[
35
]
. In particular, it is known to satisfy the relation
\mu_{i}=198\mu_{i-1}-\mu_{i-2}
, with
\mu_{0}=1,\mu_{1}=199
.

Table
10
shows the first 7 values of
\mu_{i}
and the prime divisors of the corresponding values of
M_{i}=49\mu_{i}^{2}-8
. Notice that only for
\mu_{0}=1
and
\mu_{4}=1544598001
is every prime divisor of
M_{i}
congruent to 1 mod 8, as required by Observation 4 above. So the other cases of Table
10
cannot be solutions to Equation (
189
).

i

\mu_{i}

Factorization of
M_{i}=49\mu_{i}^{2}-8

Factors mod 8

0

1

41

1

1

199

23{\cdot}239{\cdot}353

7{\cdot}7{\cdot}1

2

39401

7801199

47{\cdot}6771937{\cdot}9369319

4

1544598001

60551330795801

223{\cdot}2297{\cdot}37223{\cdot}302663{\cdot}3553471{\cdot}8761009

7{\cdot}1{\cdot}7{\cdot}7{\cdot}7{\cdot}1

Table 10.
The first 7 solutions to
m^{2}=2n^{2}-1
with
m\equiv 0\pmod{7}

Notice that for
MY^{2}=1+8Z^{2}
we have
2MY^{2}=X^{2}+2
, for
X=4Z
. By

[
29
, Theorem 5]
,
2MY^{2}=X^{2}+2
has no solution if the continued fraction expansion of
\sqrt{2M}
has odd period length. In fact, this is the case for
m=7\mu_{0}=7
(
M=41
); the continued fraction expansion of
\sqrt{82}
is
9,\overline{18}
, which has odd period length
\ell(\sqrt{82})=1
. This shows that
for
m=7
, (
189
) has no solutions. Another proof that (
189
) has no solutions for
m=7
is given by using
[
38
, Theorem 8]
or
[
41
]
. According to these results,

MY^{2}-2W^{2}=1
has no solution if
R^{2}-2MS^{2}=-1
has a solution. And in fact, for
M=41
(
m=7
),
R^{2}-2MS^{2}=-1
has the solution
R=9,S=1
.

From Table
10
, we see that the next potential solution would be for
m=7\mu_{4}
. Here, already, the numbers are very large, and we have been unable to determine the continued fraction expansion of
\sqrt{2M_{4}}
.
To see that there are no solutions for
m=7\mu_{4}
, we require a deeper result, due to Wei. As we observed above, if we have a solution
M,Y,Z
to (
189
), then

2MY^{2}=X^{2}+2
, where
X=4Z
.

Proposition 11

(
[
39
, Prop. 4.4]
)
.

Suppose that
M=p_{1}p_{2}\dots p_{j}
, where
p_{i}\equiv 1\pmod{8}
for each
i
. If the equation
2MY^{2}=X^{2}+2
has an integer solution
X,Y
, then

\prod_{i=1}^{j}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.5pt}{0}{2}{p_i}_{4}=1,

where
\big(\frac{\,\cdot\,}{\cdot}\big)_{4}
denotes the quartic residue symbol (see
[
26
, Chap. 5]
).

Recall that
\big(\frac{2}{p_{i}}\big)_{4}=\pm 1
and
\left(\frac{2}{p_{i}}\right)_{4}\equiv 2^{(p_{i}-1)/4}\pmod{p_{i}}
. From Table
10
, we have

M_{4}=p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}
, where
p_{1}=41,p_{2}=45245801,p_{3}=63018038201
. Calculations show that
\big(\frac{2}{p_{i}}\big)_{4}=-1
for
i=1,2,3
. Hence by Wei’s Proposition, there are no solutions to (
189
) for
m=7\mu_{4}
.

In fact, calculations show that for
7\leq i\leq 155
,
M_{i}
has a prime divisor congruent to 7 mod 8, so these cases also cannot be solutions to Equation (
189
). In establishing this, the only difficulty is in factorizing
M_{i}
. Once a factor congruent to 7 mod 8 has been found, it is easy to verify that it is indeed a factor. To substantiate our claim, for each
i
with
7\leq i\leq 155
we exhibit an explicit prime divisor of
M_{i}
congruent to 7 mod 8. Consider the following set of 62 primes congruent to 7 mod 8:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=\{23,47,71,79,103,167,191,223,239,263,311,359,431,479,607,719,887,

\displaystyle 983,1031,1103,1279,1399,1487,1511,1823,1879,2671,2767,3271,3559,4903,

\displaystyle 4943,6823,7583,8231,23447,39551,53527,72559,153511,167911,255511,

\displaystyle 625111,869951,1471271,2593399,10808983,13980671,39556927,108732031,

\displaystyle 125448527,160812623,209110079,627025159,9707524087,181155438071,

\displaystyle 291814585319,3072313317767,15238519898992991,39834495682679591,

\displaystyle 15327739968951498750119,110095018941508669324502008759\}.

Now consider the set

\displaystyle R=\{40,9,1,4,21,1,6,5,4,15,19,55,2,1,10,9,1,48,11,2,50,4,9,8,1,41,9,1,

\displaystyle 13,4,34,22,14,9,4,1,9,59,1,61,9,5,2,9,56,26,1,4,43,1,9,32,16,46,9,4,33,

\displaystyle 1,2,58,1,9,6,5,9,2,17,27,1,28,54,1,7,4,18,5,49,15,9,1,5,2,1,29,20,9,4,37,

\displaystyle 2,6,1,30,5,1,4,36,9,5,44,4,60,1,10,3,1,62,9,4,39,5,8,2,1,9,5,1,23,9,24,

\displaystyle 51,4,57,11,1,9,4,1,2,38,31,35,5,42,4,1,52,53,1,3,45,47,9,12,5,25,1,4,8,1\},

and let
r_{i}
denote the
i
-th member of
R
.
The enthusiastic reader will easily verify that for each
1\leq i\leq 149
, the
r_{i}
-th member of
P
is a divisor of
M_{i+6}
.

It follows from the above that the smallest possible value of
m
for which there could potentially be a solution to (
188
) and (
189
)
would have
m\geq 7\mu_{156}
. We don’t know if there is a solution for
m=7\mu_{156}
. In particular, we have been unable to find any factors of
M_{156}
, which is unsurprising as
M_{156}\cong 1.8\cdot 10^{718}
.

Remark 33
.

Note that if a solution to (
188
) and (
189
) exists, and there is an extangential LEQ corresponding to case (c) of Theorem
3
, with sides
a,b,c,d
, then by Remark
30
, its perimeter would be

2(a+b)=4mnY>4mn>2\sqrt{2}m^{2}.

In particular, if there is an extangential LEQ corresponding to
m=7\mu_{156}
, then the perimeter would be at least
5.0\cdot 10^{718}
.
